The quantum optimal transport distance between 𝜌and 𝑊𝜌𝑊† is bounded in the companion work [32] using clas- sical coarse-graining together with phase-space localization estimates (right).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous theory of quantum chaos by employing Quantum Optimal Transport (QOT) theory as a geometric foundation. It defines a quantum Lyapunov exponent that successfully avoids mathematical divergences inherent in naive generalizations. The key finding is that this quantum exponent recovers the classical maximal Lyapunov exponent in the semiclassical limit, establishing QOT as the unifying tool for the field.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, geometrically grounded tool (QOT) to quantify chaos in quantum systems, bridging the gap between classical dynamical systems theory and quantum dynamics, which is vital for understanding quantum thermalization and scrambling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of rigorously extending classical chaos theory, characterized by Lyapunov exponents, to the quantum domain due to the wavelike nature of quantum states and non-commutative phase space geometry.

**Main result.** Quantum Optimal Transport (QOT) theory provides a unifying mathematical foundation for quantum chaos, leading to a quantum Lyapunov exponent that recovers the classical maximal Lyapunov exponent in the semiclassical limit.

**Method.** The authors define quantum Lyapunov exponents by leveraging the Wasserstein distance (a metric from QOT) applied to phase-space distributions (like the Husimi distribution) to measure the divergence of quantum states over time.

**Model / system.** The framework is general, applicable to quantum systems ranging from simple few-body models to complex many-body systems, analyzed through the evolution of quantum states in phase space.

**Key observables.** Quantum Lyapunov exponents ($\lambda_q$), classical Lyapunov exponents ($\lambda_{cl}$), Out-of-Time-Order Correlators (OTOCs), and the Quantum Optimal Transport distance ($W_p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The semiclassical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$) is crucial for recovering classical results; the initial separation of states must be carefully chosen to avoid divergences.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on building upon modern noncommutative quantum optimal transport frameworks and defining admissible quantum distances satisfying specific axioms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between classical trajectory divergence, quantum state evolution as extended distributions, and how QOT provides a natural distance metric respecting non-commutativity.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the need for QOT, then defining the quantum Lyapunov exponent via the time evolution of the QOT distance, and finally proving its connection to the classical expansion rate and comparing it to OTOC diagnostics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chaos in classical systems can be characterized by Lyapunov exponents that measure the exponential divergence of nearby trajectories, but directly extending this framework to quantum mechanics has been a persistent challenge. The wavelike nature of quantum states and the non-commutative geometry of quantum phase space obstruct a straightforward generalization of classical chaos theory. Here we develop a rigorous approach to quantum chaos by leveraging quantum optimal transport theory, which provides the missing geometric foundation for measuring distances between extended quantum distributions. We define quantum Lyapunov exponents that naturally avoid divergences encountered when naïvely generalizing classical exponents, and show that in the semiclassical limit they recover the classical global expansion rate, and hence the usual maximal Lyapunov exponent when they coincide. Our framework provides a tight connection between the divergence of classical trajectories and semiclassical phase space evolution, and additionally clarifies the role of out-of-time-order correlators as diagnostics of quantum chaos. These results establish quantum optimal transport as a unifying mathematical foundation for quantum chaos theory, providing new tools to characterize dynamical behavior across the full range of quantum dynamics from simple few-body models to complex many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the gap in understanding how heterogeneous quantum technologies can form large-scale networks. It proposes a five-level hierarchical model to abstract physical platforms into reusable functionalities. The central argument is that quantum interconnects are crucial enablers that allow this abstraction, thereby enabling the definition of utility functions necessary for strategic, market-like network growth.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a high-level theoretical framework for understanding the necessary architectural evolution of quantum networks, framing interconnects not just as physical links but as abstract enablers of utility.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is developing a framework to explain the evolution of large-scale quantum networks from isolated, heterogeneous technologies into interconnected infrastructures driven by utility rather than mere technical feasibility.

**Main result.** The authors propose a hierarchical architecture (PP $ightarrow$ F $ightarrow$ S $ightarrow$ A $ightarrow$ UC) and argue that quantum interconnects are fundamental enablers that allow for the necessary abstraction to define utility functions and enable strategic, agent-based network formation.

**Method.** The work uses a conceptual modeling approach, proposing a hierarchical abstraction framework and modifying network formation models (like agent-based models) to incorporate utility derived from functional interoperability.

**Model / system.** The model describes heterogeneous quantum platforms (e.g., quantum dots, trapped ions) connected by quantum interconnects. The system is conceptualized through a five-level hierarchy: Physical Platforms, Functionalities, Services, Applications, and Use-Cases.

**Key observables.** Utility functions ($U_i$), interoperability parameters ($I_{ij}$), and the degree of abstraction achieved at the functionality level.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the utility function, which must be defined independently of the underlying physical platform.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that quantum interconnects provide sufficient interoperability to decouple network functionalities from specific physical implementations, thereby enabling utility-driven decision-making.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from a technology-driven strategic landscape to an expected utility-driven landscape, supported by the proposed hierarchical model.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (technology-driven growth), proposes the hierarchical abstraction framework (PP to UC), develops the role of interconnects in enabling functional decoupling, and concludes by showing how this enables utility-based, agent-driven network formation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The realization of large-scale quantum networks requires more than advances in quantum repeaters, memories, and processors, it requires a framework explaining how heterogeneous quantum technologies evolve from isolated deployments into interconnected infrastructures. While the classical Internet evolved under strong utility incentives associated with resource sharing and communication demands, quantum networking currently lacks dominant applications capable of generating comparable incentives. As a consequence, contemporary quantum networks are largely formed through technology-driven decisions motivated by technical feasibility, experimental validation, and expected future value. This work argues that the absence of utility-driven network formation is not solely a consequence of immature applications, but also of insufficient abstraction. In particular, heterogeneous quantum platforms remain tightly coupled to the functionalities they provide, preventing the definition of technology-independent utility functions. A hierarchical architecture consisting of Physical Platforms (PP), Functionalities (F), Services (S), Applications (A), and Use-Cases (UC) is proposed, together with the argument that quantum interconnects constitute the enabling technology required to decouple physical implementations from network functionalities. Such decoupling permits the definition of utility functions at the functionality level and establishes the conditions under which strategic (agent-based) network formation becomes applicable. Quantum interconnects should therefore be viewed not only as interoperability devices, but also as fundamental enablers of strategic quantum network evolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes Random Recursive Simplicial Complexes (RRSC), stochastic models for higher-dimensional random networks. By deriving recurrence relations and solving them using ODE approximations, the authors determine the asymptotic linear growth laws for the average number of simplices of various dimensions. This provides deep insights into the topological scaling of complex, randomly built structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on pure combinatorics/topology, the study of random complexes and their spectral dimension relates to the geometry of complex systems, which can inform models of quantum entanglement structure or network connectivity in quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the asymptotic growth laws and structural properties of random recursive simplicial complexes (RRSC), which are stochastic models for higher-dimensional random networks.

**Main result.** The average number of simplices $\langle S_d angle$ grows linearly with the number of vertices $N$, and the analysis yields specific amplitudes for this growth law for low dimensions.

**Method.** The analysis relies on deriving recurrence relations for the average number of simplices and approximating these discrete recurrences with Ordinary Differential Equations (ODEs) to find asymptotic solutions.

**Model / system.** The model is the Random Recursive Simplicial Complex (RRSC(m)), which grows by iteratively adding a vertex and connecting it to a randomly chosen existing simplex. It is related to Random Recursive Trees (RRT) and Hypergraphs.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are $S_d$, the number of $d$-simplices, and their average $\langle S_d angle$ and variance $	ext{Var}[S_d]$. The Euler characteristic $\chi = \sum (-1)^d S_d$ is also a key invariant.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $m$ of the complex, and the number of vertices $N$. The analysis focuses on the asymptotic limit $N 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes asymptotic self-averaging, allowing the use of average values $\langle S_d angle$ to characterize the system's behavior for large $N$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction steps of the RRSC(2) model for small $N$, show the concentration and self-averaging of $S_1$ for RRSC(2), and demonstrate the numerical convergence of calculated amplitudes for various dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the growth of $\langle S_d angle$ and $	ext{Var}[S_d]$ by deriving recurrence relations, solving them via ODE approximations, and comparing the results for different dimensions $m$. It also examines related models like the Homogeneous Simplicial Complex (HSC).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate random recursive simplicial complexes growing by adding, at each step, a vertex together with a simplex formed by joining the new vertex with a randomly chosen existing simplex. We also add all faces of the new simplex to ensure that the resulting object remains a simplicial complex. If the choice of an existing simplex is uniform among simplices of dimension $<m$, the number $S_d$ of simplices of any admissible dimension $d\leq m$ is an asymptotically self-averaging random variable. This feature allows us to determine the asymptotic growth law of the average of $S_d$ when the number of vertices diverges. We also probe the degree distribution, examine the probabilities of various extreme outcomes, and analyze the characteristics of the first vertex.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how applying tensile strain tunes the electronic and magnetic properties of the iron-based superconductor FeTe. Using DFT+DMFT and Heisenberg models, the authors reveal a strain-driven pathway that causes the magnetic order to evolve from bicollinear to stripe antiferromagnetism. This magnetic change is intrinsically linked to an orbital-selective reconstruction of the electronic structure, providing a unified picture of correlated behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed, quantitative mapping of how external strain tunes the interplay between electronic correlations (orbital selectivity) and magnetic order in a strongly correlated material, which is a key theme in modern condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a complete, unified, strain-driven trajectory connecting the magnetic and correlated electronic states of FeTe, specifically addressing the orbital-selective reconstruction and the evolution from bicollinear to stripe antiferromagnetism (AFM).

**Main result.** Tensile strain drives a pronounced orbital-selective reconstruction and a magnetic transition trajectory from bicollinear to stripe AFM via an intermediate staggered n-mer AFM regime.

**Method.** The study combines Density Functional Theory plus Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DFT+DMFT) calculations with Heisenberg model analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is FeTe, a parent material among iron-based superconductors, studied under systematically varied in-plane biaxial tensile strain.

**Key observables.** Momentum-resolved spectral functions, imaginary part of the Matsubara self-energy, local spin susceptibility, and local spin moment ($M_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tensile strain (ranging from small to large values), and the magnetic coupling parameters ($J_1, J_2, J_3$) derived from the Heisenberg model.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on DFT+DMFT to capture correlated electronic structure effects and an effective Heisenberg model to map the magnetic phase transitions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral evolution under strain, showing changes in coherence and spectral weight, alongside plots detailing the evolution of magnetic susceptibilities and local spin moments across different strain regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the effect of tensile strain on FeTe, first using DFT+DMFT to track orbital reconstruction and correlation strength, and then coupling these electronic findings to the magnetic phase diagram derived from Heisenberg model analysis, establishing the full strain-driven trajectory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

FeTe, as a representative parent material among iron-based superconductors, provides an ideal platform for exploring the interplay among orbital-selective correlations, magnetism, and unconventional superconductivity. However, a unified picture of the correlated electronic structure and magnetism of FeTe under strain remains to be fully clarified. Here, combining density functional theory plus dynamical mean-field theory and Heisenberg model analysis, we uncover an orbital-selective reconstruction of the correlated electronic structure and reveal a strain-driven trajectory from bicollinear to stripe antiferromagnetism (AFM) via an intermediate competing staggered $n$-mer AFM regime in FeTe. Moderate strain gives rise to a regime where more coherent quasiparticles coexist with suppressed local moments. Further strain drives FeTe into an incoherent correlated regime with robust local moments and Fe-$3d_{z^2}$-dominated low-energy states. These results establish a strain-driven trajectory across distinct magnetic and correlated electronic states in FeTe.

</details>

### [Strong Converse Exponent of Quantum State Merging](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27202v1)

**Authors:** Mario Berta, Hao-Chung Cheng, Roberto Rubboli, Marco Tomamichel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.27202v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2608.27202_figures/2608.27202_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Illustration of the quantum state merging task protocol. Left: Alice and Bob share n copies of a tripartite pure state |ψ⟩⊗n ABR alongside preshared entanglement of Schmidt rank |Kn|. Right: After applying a one-way local operation and classical communication (LOCC) protocol, Alice’s system An is merged at Bob’s side, consuming or distilling entanglement such that the final entangled state has a Schmidt rank of |Ln|.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper calculates the exact strong converse exponent for quantum state merging, a key task in quantum information theory. It establishes that this exponent is governed by optimized $\alpha$-$z$ conditional Rényi entropies, which differs from standard results. The findings provide rigorous bounds on the achievable rates for quantum information transfer protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The precise characterization of the strong converse exponent using specific conditional entropy forms provides deep insights into the fundamental limits of quantum information processing tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the strong converse exponent for the entanglement cost associated with quantum state merging.

**Main result.** The strong converse exponent is characterized by optimized $\alpha$-$z$ conditional Rényi entropies, contrasting with typical results governed by sandwiched conditional Rényi entropies.

**Method.** The analysis adapts the club-sandwiched converse method and utilizes techniques from Schatten-norm Hölder theory to establish asymptotic bounds on the fidelity decay.

**Model / system.** The study concerns the quantum state merging task, where a tripartite pure state $\psi_{ABR}$ is used to transfer information from Alice (A) to Bob, preserving coherence relative to an inaccessible reference system R.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Cost ($r$), Squared Fidelity ($F$), Purified Distance, and various conditional Rényi entropies ($H^{\uparrow}_{\alpha, \alpha/2}(A|B)_{\psi}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The exponent depends on the optimization parameter $\alpha \in [1/2, 1]$ and the rate $r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and focuses on the strong converse regime where the optimal fidelity decays exponentially.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the quantum state merging protocol. Table I summarizes the strong converse and error exponents for different smoothing types (Globally vs. Partially smoothed).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the result by establishing upper and lower bounds on the fidelity decay rate, comparing the constraints imposed by global vs. partial smoothing, and deriving the final exponent using variational representations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the strong converse exponent for the entanglement cost of quantum state merging, showing that it is characterized by the optimized $α$-$z$ conditional Rényi entropies with $z=α/2\in[1/2,1]$. This contrasts with the sandwiched conditional Rényi entropies that typically govern strong converse exponents in quantum information theory. As a consequence, we derive the strong converse exponent of the partially smoothed conditional min-entropy in purified distance. This exponent is governed by club-sandwiched conditional entropies, whereas global smoothing leads to a sandwiched expression.

</details>

**Authors:** Ning Bao, Jacob March  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.26377v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work refines the understanding of holographic entropy by replacing problematic, dimension-dependent bounds with the capacity of entanglement. It proves that this capacity provides a robust, dimension-free constraint on the entanglement structure of quantum states. This is crucial for understanding the fundamental limits of entropy estimation in quantum gravity settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, resource-aware bounds on entanglement entropy derived from gravity/holography, which informs the fundamental limits of quantum information processing in strongly coupled systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses ambiguities in applying holographic entropy formulas, like Ryu-Takayanagi, to finite resources by refining the theoretical bounds on entanglement entropy.

**Main result.** It establishes that the capacity of entanglement provides a dimension-free, saturated bound, and shows that the minimal surface fixes all one-shot Rényi entropies simultaneously for holographic states.

**Method.** The analysis involves replacing dimension-dependent factors with the capacity of entanglement (variance of modular energy) and using concentration inequalities derived from the modular Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The framework is based on the AdS/CFT correspondence, relating boundary von Neumann entropy to the area of minimal surfaces in the bulk geometry.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entropy ($S_{vN}$), Rényi entropies ($S_\alpha$), and the capacity of entanglement ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entanglement area, the central charge, and the small perturbation scale relative to $S_{vN}^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes local smoothness of the remainder in the Rényi spectrum and relies on the holographic input structure for concentration estimates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by identifying the ambiguities in the RT formula, proposing the capacity of entanglement as a replacement bound, and then demonstrating how this leads to strong constraints on the state's one-shot entropy spectrum.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Ryu-Takayanagi formula equates the area of a minimal surface with the von Neumann entropy of a boundary subregion, and leaves two things about that identification open.   The first is how sharply a geometry fixes an entropy. Fannes-Audenaert answers with a Hilbert-space dimension, which diverges as the cutoff is removed however close the two states are. We replace it with the capacity of entanglement, the variance of the modular energy, whose square root grows like the square root of the entangling area where the dimensional factor grows like the regulated volume. The bound is dimension-free and saturated, and it makes the ambiguity of the entropy subextensive for any perturbation whose capacity is small compared with $S_{vN}^2$ times the trace norm.   The second is what the area means for a single state, since compression and dilution rates are defined only for many copies while a geometry describes one. When a single replica saddle dominates near $α= 1$, every smooth Rényi entropy at fixed $α> 1$ agrees with $S_{vN}$ to $\textit{O}(\sqrt{S_{vN}})$, as do the smooth min- and max-entropies. The minimal surface therefore fixes every one-shot entropy of the state at once, with large central charge playing the role of large copy number in the asymptotic equipartition property.   As a consequence we bound how far outside the holographic entropy cone a holographic state can appear to fall, leaving estimation and certification open.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, highly efficient quantum algorithm for decoding quantum expander codes. It significantly improves upon existing methods by achieving linear time complexity and logarithmic parallel depth. The development is crucial for advancing fault-tolerant quantum computation by enabling the correction of a larger fraction of errors in these quantum codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly efficient theoretical tool for quantum error correction, which is fundamental to building fault-tolerant quantum computers, directly impacting the feasibility of large-scale quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a faster and more efficient quantum adaptation of the 'Find Erasures and Decode' algorithm for quantum expander codes.

**Main result.** The new 'Find Rows and Columns and Decode' algorithm runs in linear time and is parallelizable to logarithmic depth, correcting significantly more errors than previous methods.

**Method.** The method adapts classical decoding techniques by reformulating the decoding process using iterative subgraphs and peeling procedures derived from the graph structure of the expander code.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum CSS codes derived from lossless expander bipartite graphs, which are used to construct quantum expander codes for error correction.

**Key observables.** Error correction capability (number of correctable errors), time complexity (linear time, logarithmic depth), and the required expansion parameter $\delta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lossless expansion parameter $\delta$, the maximum degree $d$, and the blocklength $\ell$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires the lossless expansion parameter $\delta < 1/3$ for the presented algorithm to function optimally.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the quantum expander code structure, introducing the 'Find Rows and Columns and Decode' algorithm, detailing the steps involving row/column support computation and peeling, and concluding with a rigorous complexity analysis for both sequential and parallel execution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A new adaptation of the Find Erasures and Decode algorithm from classical to quantum expander codes is presented. It runs in linear time and is parallelizable to logarithmic depth. Compared to Small Set Flip and Small Set Find, the new algorithm avoids the overhead of considering the subsets of stabilizer generators, requires less expansion and corrects more errors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical gap in evaluating distributed quantum circuit partitioning by moving beyond simple entanglement cost metrics. Using an automated pipeline, the authors systematically test various algorithms on simulated DQC architectures across different network topologies. The findings prove that structural metrics, such as circuit depth and gate density, are crucial for accurately predicting real-world performance penalties in modular quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on quantifying physical overheads like circuit depth and gate density in a distributed setting is highly relevant for understanding the practical limitations of scaling quantum computation beyond single, monolithic chips.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Current evaluations of Distributed Quantum Computing (DQC) circuit partitioning algorithms are insufficient because they focus too narrowly on total entanglement cost, ignoring crucial structural and temporal overheads from network constraints.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that partitioning algorithms with similar entanglement costs can yield drastically different physical execution penalties, emphasizing that structure-aware metrics are essential for guiding DQC compiler design.

**Method.** The authors developed an automated, reproducible evaluation pipeline that applies established monolithic benchmarking metrics to partitioned distributed circuits across various quantum network topologies and standardized workloads.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is Distributed Quantum Computing (DQC), which networks modular Quantum Processing Units (QPUs). Circuits are represented in OpenQASM format and are mapped onto specified network topologies (e.g., linear, grid, fully connected).

**Key observables.** Inter-QPU communication bottlenecks, circuit depth inflation, gate density reduction, e-bit count, circuit depth ratio, and $\Delta$gate density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Standardized workloads include QASMBench, Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT), and QAOA; topologies include 4-QPU linear, grid, and fully connected networks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The evaluation assumes that intra-QPU connectivity is fully connected, and the analysis relies on applying monolithic metrics to distributed structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evaluation pipeline, compare reproduced e-bit counts across benchmarks (QFT, QAOA, etc.), and show correlation matrices mapping input circuit properties against DQC partitioning costs and structural penalties.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the evaluation gap, details the automated pipeline for testing various partitioning algorithms (MLFM-R, FGP-rOEE, Pytket variants) across multiple topologies and workloads, presents empirical results comparing structural penalties, and concludes that comprehensive metrics are necessary for compiler design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed Quantum Computing (DQC) addresses the physical scaling limitations of monolithic quantum processors by networking modular Quantum Processing Units (QPUs). Efficient execution of quantum algorithms on DQC architectures requires compiling them across QPUs while minimizing inter-QPU communication bottlenecks, primarily through circuit partitioning. However, current evaluations of state-of-the-art partitioning heuristics focus primarily on the total entanglement cost of the partitions, failing to capture the broader structural and temporal overheads introduced by distributed network constraints. This paper addresses this evaluation gap by applying established monolithic benchmarking metrics to partitioned distributed circuits to quantify the performance impact of network constraints. Using an open-source, automated evaluation pipeline, we systematically assess diverse partitioning algorithms across standardized workloads and quantum network topologies. Our empirical results reveal that partitioning algorithms with comparable entanglement costs can still introduce drastically different physical execution penalties. By exposing these hidden trade-offs, such as severe increases in circuit depth and substantial reductions in gate density, this study demonstrates that comprehensive circuit-level metrics are essential for guiding the future design of DQC compilers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously establishes a sharp phase transition for the problem of fitting random data points with an ellipsoid in high dimensions. It determines a critical threshold $\alpha^\star(\kappa)$ that dictates whether an exact fit exists, revealing a universality property dependent only on the fourth moment of the data distribution. This resolves a long-standing conjecture for Gaussian data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical framework of phase transitions in high-dimensional geometry and optimization is highly relevant to understanding the generalization capabilities and phase transitions in complex machine learning models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the existence and nature of a sharp phase transition for determining if a set of random vectors can be perfectly fitted by a positive definite ellipsoid.

**Main result.** A sharp threshold $\alpha^\star(\kappa)$ exists, separating the regime where a fit is possible from the regime where the minimum fitting error is strictly positive. For Gaussian data, this threshold is found to be $1/4$.

**Method.** The analysis uses tools from random matrix theory and convex optimization, framing the problem as a random Semidefinite Program (SDP) in both satisfiable (SAT) and unsatisfiable (UNSAT) regimes.

**Model / system.** The model consists of $n$ random vectors $\{x_i\} \subset \mathbb{R}^d$ with independent subgaussian coordinates, mean zero, and a common fourth moment $\kappa$. The problem is finding $R \succeq 0$ such that $x_i^	op R x_i = 1$ for all $i$.

**Key observables.** The ratio $\alpha = n/d^2$, the threshold $\alpha^\star(\kappa)$, and the optimal squared fitting error $\Gamma_X$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The common fourth moment $\kappa$ of the coordinate distribution, and the dimension $d$ relative to the number of samples $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The vectors are assumed to have independent coordinates satisfying subgaussian bounds, though later results extend universality to broader classes of distributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing the theoretical threshold $\alpha^\star(\kappa)$ versus numerical estimates, and the average squared loss in the unsatisfiable regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results by analyzing the SAT and UNSAT regimes separately, deriving the threshold $\alpha^\star(\kappa)$ based on the fourth moment $\kappa$, and proving universality for the asymptotic error in the failure regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a sharp phase transition for fitting random vectors by an ellipsoid. The random vectors have independent subgaussian coordinates with mean zero, variance one, and a common fourth moment, and the number of vectors is proportional to the square of the dimension. We identify an explicit satisfiability threshold such that, with high probability, a positive definite ellipsoid passes through every data point below the threshold, whereas no positive semidefinite fit exists above it. We also determine the optimal squared fitting error throughout the unsatisfiable regime. In particular, the threshold depends on the coordinate distributions only through their common fourth moment, revealing a fourth moment universality phenomenon. For standard Gaussian data the threshold is $1/4$, resolving the ellipsoid fitting conjecture.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a mechanism for achieving a Josephson diode effect in a symmetric junction by engineering the non-equilibrium occupation of quantum states via attached reservoirs. By analyzing the system using advanced Green's function techniques, the authors demonstrate that the diode asymmetry originates solely from the reservoir-induced occupation factor, not from intrinsic junction asymmetry. This provides a powerful tool for designing superconducting electronic components.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated theoretical framework for engineering non-reciprocity in superconducting devices using reservoir engineering, which is highly relevant to superconducting quantum circuits and mesoscopic physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a novel mechanism for generating a Josephson diode effect in a symmetric junction, where the nonreciprocity arises purely from non-equilibrium occupation of current-carrying states rather than an inherent asymmetry in the Andreev spectrum.

**Main result.** The diode effect is shown to be entirely governed by a reservoir-controlled occupation factor ($P_\gamma$), which can be engineered to approach ideal rectification ($\eta_D 	o 1$) even when the underlying spectral current ($I_A$) is reciprocal.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh-Lindblad formalism, first in the simplified Quantum Zeno limit, and later extended to finite superconducting gaps and dissipation using contour-ordered Green's functions.

**Model / system.** The system is a double-quantum-dot junction coupled to superconducting leads and engineered reservoirs. The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation, which is analyzed using Green's function techniques.

**Key observables.** Diode efficiency ($\eta_D$), critical currents ($I_c^+, |I_c^-|$), and the current factorization $I_{ss} = I_A P_\gamma$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reservoir coupling strength ($\kappa$), superconducting gap ($\Delta$), and local Coulomb repulsion ($U$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis relies on the Quantum Zeno limit ($\kappa \gg t, \Gamma$), but the results are shown to be robust and extend to finite $\kappa$ and finite $\Delta$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Zeno filtering separating bright/dark orbitals, the diode effect via occupation reweighting ($P_\gamma$), and the effect of Coulomb repulsion inducing a dark-pair resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from the simplified Zeno limit analysis to deriving the full steady-state Green's functions using contour formalism, culminating in the characterization of the finite-gap diode efficiency.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a Josephson diode mechanism in which nonreciprocity arises not from a conventional asymmetric Andreev spectrum but from nonequilibrium occupation of the current-carrying states engineered by attached reservoirs. We realize this mechanism in a double-quantum-dot junction, where a phase-textured nonlocal reservoir acts as a quantum Zeno selector: rapid dissipation freezes out the bright state directly coupled to the jump operator $L$, while preserving an orthogonal dark Andreev channel whose supercurrent remains comparable to that of the lossless junction. In the infinite-gap limit, the steady-state current factorizes as $I_{\rm ss}=I_A P_γ$, so that even when the Andreev current $I_A$ is strictly reciprocal, the phase asymmetry of $P_γ$ alone can produce a Josephson diode effect through reservoir engineering. We further show that local Coulomb repulsion can drive the system toward a nearly ideal diode regime via a dark-pair resonance. Using Keldysh-Lindblad calculations, we demonstrate that our results remain robust for realistic junctions with a finite superconducting gap and dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect in a chain of dipoles coupled via long-range radiative interactions within a chiral medium. The authors find that the resulting skin mode profile has a unique decay structure involving both an exponential pole contribution and a slower, logarithmic tail. This work advances the understanding of boundary effects in open, chiral, and long-range coupled quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the study of NHSE into the realm of long-range radiative coupling in chiral media, providing a detailed profile of the boundary localization that goes beyond simple exponential decay, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems and photonic structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect (NHSE) in a system characterized by long-range radiative coupling within a reciprocal chiral medium.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a complex skin mode profile composed of an exponential term from a complex-beta pole and a longer-range tail decaying as 1/[n(ln n)^2].

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing the open-boundary condition (OBC) spectrum to the Bloch spectrum using complex analysis techniques, particularly analyzing the complex-beta plane structure.

**Model / system.** The model is a straight chain of electric dipoles embedded in a passive reciprocal chiral medium, whose coupling is mediated by long-range radiative interactions.

**Key observables.** OBC spectrum vs. Bloch spectrum, skin mode profile decay rates, and the contraction of the OBC spectrum toward the Bloch spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chirality parameter (kappa), lattice site distance (n), and the chain length (N).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis notes that the standard equal-modulus construction is insufficient because the finite-chain decay is ultimately limited by light-line branch cuts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Bloch spectrum versus the equal-modulus locus, show representative eigenvector amplitudes exhibiting different decay types, and compare OBC eigenvalues to the Bloch spectrum for increasing chain lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon established theory of NHSE, analyzing the governing equations derived from the dipole interaction and radiative coupling. It proceeds by calculating and comparing the OBC and Bloch spectra, deriving the asymptotic decay profile, and discussing the implications of the light-line branch cuts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We find that long-range radiative coupling through a passive reciprocal chiral medium produces an unusual non-Hermitian skin effect in a chain of electric dipoles. Although the system is reciprocal, the chirality generates polarization-dependent phase accumulation and attenuation, leading to degenerate pairs of skin modes localized at opposite boundaries. We find that the skin mode profile consists of an exponential contribution from an isolated complex-$β$ pole and a longer-range tail proportional to $1/[n(\ln n)^2]$, where $n$ is the distance from the occupied boundary measured in lattice sites. Finite-chain calculations show that part of the OBC spectrum contracts toward the Bloch spectrum, whereas the number of boundary-localized modes remains extensive.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys cavity solitons, stable light spots in nonlinear optical cavities, detailing their mathematical modeling using equations like the Lugiato-Lefever equation. The authors establish that these solitons possess unique robustness and plasticity, making them promising candidates for next-generation all-optical memory and processing units.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics, as cavity solitons represent stable, localized excitations in nonlinear wave systems, analogous to engineered quantum states or information carriers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reviews the history, physical interpretation, and potential applications of cavity solitons—stable, bright, non-diffracting spots of light in driven optical cavities.

**Main result.** Cavity solitons can exist even when the nonlinear medium properties oppose those needed for standard Kerr solitons, suggesting they are robust candidates for all-optical information processing.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving and solving mean-field models, such as the Lugiato-Lefever equation, and performing stability analysis using Jacobian eigenvalues.

**Model / system.** The system is a driven optical cavity containing a nonlinear medium, modeled by perturbed Nonlinear Schrödinger Equations (NLS) or the Lugiato-Lefever (LL) equation. These models describe the spatial evolution of light fields.

**Key observables.** Soliton stability (eigenvalues of the Jacobian), field profiles (localized spots), and the existence domain of stable solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Linear loss ($\epsilon$), cavity mistuning ($	heta$), and pump intensity ($I$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations, particularly the high-finesse assumption used to derive the LL equation from the full cavity dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic memory systems, Gaussian beam switching, bifurcation diagrams showing soliton states vs. parameters, and phase-plane plots confirming soliton stability regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by reviewing the historical context (Stone Age to Information Age), developing the mathematical models (NLS to LL), analyzing the stability and physical interpretations of the solitons, and concluding with potential applications in all-optical processing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A driven optical cavity containing a nonlinear medium can support stable, soliton-like objects, bright non-diffracting spots of light on a uniform background. Importantly, the nonlinear medium need not support ordinary (propagating) spatial solitons, and cavity solitons have been both predicted and observed in a saturable absorber, and predicted to even exist in self-defocusing media. The history, present status, and physical interpretation of such cavity solitons is reviewed. Recent research predicts cavity solitons in semiconductor cavity models with properties interesting for applications to optical information processing. Cavity solitons are natural `bits' for spatial information, possessing both robustness and plasticity, and some potential applications of their unique properties are mentioned.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how breaking inversion symmetry in two-band superconductors stabilizes interband phase solitons (IBPS) in a ring geometry. The key mechanism is a magneto-electric coupling term that acts as a chiral bias, forcing the soliton to become the true ground state. This transition is predicted to manifest as a unique, field-odd signature in the measured current-flux response.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of stabilizing a topological excitation (the soliton) via a symmetry-breaking coupling term ($\Lambda$) is analogous to controlling quantum states in superconducting circuits or Josephson junctions, making it relevant to open quantum systems and topological physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines the conditions under which an Interband Phase Soliton (IBPS) in a two-band superconducting ring transitions from a metastable state to a true ground state.

**Main result.** Breaking inversion symmetry introduces a magneto-electric coupling term that biases the energy balance, allowing the soliton to become the true ground state, which is observable as a field-odd branch in current-flux measurements.

**Method.** The analysis involves minimizing a reduced Gibbs energy functional derived from the two-band Ginzburg-Landau theory, solving the Double Sine-Gordon equation, and analyzing the resulting phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-band superconductor confined to a ring geometry. The physics is modeled using a GL functional augmented by a magneto-electric Lifshitz term, leading to a relative-phase degree of freedom ($\phi$) that supports soliton excitations.

**Key observables.** Field-odd soliton branches in the equilibrium current-flux response $I_n(\Phi_a)$; the ground state selection between uniform and solitonic configurations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magneto-electric coupling strength ($\Lambda$), the winding number ($n$), and the superconducting gap parameters ($g_1, g_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a thin-walled ring geometry and that the order parameters are rigid, depending only on the azimuthal coordinate $\phi$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup in a noncentrosymmetric ring, the double-sine-Gordon profile for the soliton, and phase diagrams showing the transition between uniform and solitonic ground states as the bias $\Lambda$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and the problem of metastability in centrosymmetric rings, to incorporating the symmetry-breaking magneto-electric term ($\Lambda$), deriving the energy minimization conditions, and finally predicting the observable signature in current-flux measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-band superconductors maintain a relative interband phase which can carry winding soliton excitations in a superconducting ring, supported by independent winding numbers in the two bands. In rings of superconductors obeying the inversion symmetry the interband phase solitons are metastable states, separated from the uniform ground state by the energy of screening currents. In this work we find that, breaking the inversion symmetry strongly enough, one can make the soliton a true ground state. In that case, a magneto-electric coupling, absent in centrosymmetric materials, contributes critically above a certain threshold, a relevant, free-energy term, odd with respect to the winding number, which biases the energy balance in favor of a particular winding sign. Once the bias outweighs the energy cost that originally made the soliton metastable, a phase soliton with a finite winding number becomes the ground state, with chirality set by the applied field. In current--flux measurements performed in equilibrium states, the effect is demonstrated by field-odd soliton branches, that replace the metastable ones existing in mesoscopic rings, built by two-component superconductors, realizing a magneto-electric analog of the Little--Parks fluxoid-branch physics in the interband relative-phase sector.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work addresses the challenge of distinguishing true Majorana zero modes in a topological superconductor from spurious signals in a normal wire segment. By analyzing the stability of the quantum capacitance response across a range of independently controlled parameters, the authors demonstrate that only the topological system exhibits the required robust, persistent signature, providing a clear experimental benchmark.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, experimentally testable diagnostic tool—parametric stability—to confirm the topological nature of superconducting states, which is vital for realizing topological quantum computing hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to experimentally distinguish the quantum capacitance signatures arising from a true Topological Majorana Wire (TS wire) versus those from a gapless Normal Wire Segment, as both can exhibit misleading Majorana-like flux-dependent oscillations.

**Main result.** The topological response is characterized by the persistence of the two parity branches over a finite range of independently controlled parameters, a stability criterion that the gapless normal wire segment fails to meet.

**Method.** The authors calculated parity-resolved quantum capacitance ($\Delta C_Q$) by analyzing the system's response to varying external parameters ($\mu, V_{QD}$) in both theoretical models.

**Model / system.** The study models a quantum-dot interferometer coupled to either a superconducting topological wire or a gapless normal wire segment. The analysis involves calculating the quantum capacitance using parity-resolved methods based on the system's effective Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Parity-resolved quantum capacitance ($\Delta C_Q$), flux-dependent oscillations, and the stability/persistence of the two parity branches over a finite parameter range.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dot potential ($V_{QD}$), chemical potential ($\mu$), magnetic flux, and temperature ($T=50$ mK).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The distinction relies on the parametric stability of the response over a finite region, rather than matching the signal at a single optimized point in parameter space.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare parity-resolved capacitance maps for both gapless and topological interferometers, showing the separation of maxima (e.g., 35.0 $\mu$eV vs. 5.25 $\mu$eV) and the stability of the response under parameter sweeps.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of mimicking Majorana signatures, develops the theoretical framework using parity-resolved quantum capacitance, calculates and compares the signatures for the gapless and topological cases, and concludes by emphasizing the necessity of parameter-space stability for definitive identification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Majorana zero modes (MZMs) are spatially separated, near-zero-energy excitations expected at the ends of a topological superconducting (TS) wire. A quantum-dot interferometer can be used to probe the quantum capacitance of the TS wire, and the location and magnitude of the capacitance resonances provide information about the MZMs, while their magnetic flux dependence probes coherent coupling to the two Majorana modes. It has been recently shown that, a gapless (i.e., $Δ=0$) wire segment can also exhibit flux-dependent quantum capacitance oscillations through Aharonov-Bohm interference and, with suitable tuning, can reproduce a Majorana-like response. Here we show that the two mechanisms can be distinguished experimentally. In the gapless normal wire segment, the two parity-dependent signals originate from separate energy resonances corresponding to the lack of generic zero-energy states. By contrast, in the topological wire, the pair of low energy levels with even and odd parity are nearly degenerate in energy, and therefore, the two parity branches remain within the same broader resonance region in the quantum-dot potential, and persist under independent variations of the dot potential and wire chemical potential. Our results show that the experimentally observed quantum capacitance response is distinguished not by a particular flux trace at one optimized point in parameter space, but by its persistence over a finite range of independently controlled parameters, including the quantum-dot potential. This parameter space stability provides a direct means of ruling out the gapless normal wire segment as the origin of the observed Majorana-like response.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models quantum transport through a quantum dot coupled to Majorana bound states in a superconducting nanowire. By analyzing the linear conductance, the authors predict distinct oscillatory behaviors dependent on magnetic flux and bias voltage asymmetries. The findings emphasize that these asymmetry parameters are critical factors that must be accounted for when interpreting experimental measurements of MBS signatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit dependence of transport signatures on asymmetry parameters ($\alpha, q$) provides crucial, testable predictions for experimental realization and characterization of topological qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum transport signatures of Majorana Bound States (MBSs) through a quantum dot coupled asymmetrically to a topological superconducting nanowire junction.

**Main result.** The linear conductance exhibits characteristic oscillations dependent on the magnetic flux phase ($\phi$) and the bias voltage asymmetry parameter ($q$), with periodicity changing based on whether the central QD energy level is tuned to the Fermi level.

**Method.** The analysis employs non-equilibrium Green's function techniques to calculate the linear conductance ($G$) in the subgap regime.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a central quantum dot (QD) coupled asymmetrically to normal leads and to two MBSs localized at the ends of a topological superconducting nanowire threaded by a tunable magnetic flux. The Hamiltonian includes terms for leads, MBSs, the QD, and tunneling.

**Key observables.** Linear conductance ($G$), which is analyzed by decomposing it into electron tunneling ($G_{	ext{ET}}$), local Andreev reflection ($G_{	ext{LAR}}$), and crossed Andreev reflection ($G_{	ext{CAR}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Leads–QD coupling asymmetry ($\alpha$), bias voltage asymmetry ($q$), magnetic flux phase ($\phi$), and the QD energy level ($\epsilon_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the zero-temperature and finite-temperature limits, assuming the low-energy transport regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically represent the junction setup and illustrate the dependence of conductance on parameters like $\phi$, $T$, $\alpha$, and $q$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using Green's functions, analyzes the conductance components ($G_{	ext{ET}}, G_{	ext{LAR}}, G_{	ext{CAR}}$) separately, and presents results for both unhybridized and hybridized MBSs in both zero and finite temperature regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study of the quantum transport through a nanoscale system in which a central quantum dot (QD) is coupled asymmetrically to normal leads and to two Majorana bound states (MBSs) localized at the ends of a topological superconducting nanowire threaded by a tunable magnetic flux. The effects of the leads--QD coupling asymmetry parameter $α$ and the bias voltage asymmetry parameter $q$ on the system's linear conductance are considered for the case of unhybridized and hybridized MBSs. In the zero-temperature limit, for unhybridized MBSs the system's linear conductance is finite only when the magnetic flux phase $φ= (2n+1)π$ ($n\in\mathbb{Z}$) and it scales as $\mathcal{G}=2qαe^2/[h(α+1)]$, while for hybridized MBSs it presents a complicated dependence on the system's parameters. At finite temperature, for unhybridized MBSs, the system's linear conductance oscillates as a function of the magnetic flux phase $φ$ with a period of $2π$, and the position of the linear conductance maxima can be shifted from $φ=2nπ$ to $φ=(2n+1)π$ by simply varying the value of the bias voltage asymmetry parameter $q$. For hybridized MBSs, the conductance exhibits a similar behavior when the energy level of the central QD, $\varepsilon_d$, is tuned at the leads' Fermi level ($\varepsilon_d=\varepsilon_F$), although when $\varepsilon_d\neq\varepsilon_F$ the oscillation period changes to $4π$, and the position of the linear conductance maxima depends on the actual value of $\varepsilon_d$ and other parameters in the system. Our results highlight the experimental importance of the leads-QD and bias voltage asymmetry parameters, which are often present in realistic experimental setups, and can strongly affect the identification and observation of MBSs transport signatures.

</details>

</div>

**Summary.** This experimental work measures the charge of quasiparticles in even-denominator Fractional Quantum Hall states within bilayer graphene using a gate-defined antidot. The key finding is the observation of a localized charge of $e/4$, which strongly suggests that these states possess a non-Abelian topological ground state. The study utilizes precise measurements of oscillation periodicities to confirm these fractional charges.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation of fractional charge and non-Abelian statistics in a solid-state platform like graphene is highly relevant to understanding exotic correlated electron states and potential topological quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to measure the charge of quasiparticles hosted by even-denominator Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) states to determine the underlying topology of the ground state.

**Main result.** The authors observed localized quasiparticle charges of $e/4$ at several even-denominator FQH states ($
u = -5/2, -1/2, 3/2$), suggesting compatibility with a non-Abelian ground state.

**Method.** Measurements were performed using a gate-defined antidot in bilayer graphene, analyzing oscillations in transmitted resistance ($\delta R_t$) as a function of gate voltage and magnetic field.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Bernal-stacked bilayer graphene (BLG) containing a gate-defined antidot, which hosts the FQH liquid in the surrounding region.

**Key observables.** Localized quasiparticle charge ($q$), oscillation periodicities in gate voltage ($\Delta V$) and magnetic field ($\Delta B$), and the filling factor ($
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Even-denominator filling factors ($
u = -5/2, -1/2, 3/2$), the coupling strength between antidot-bound states and edge states, and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extracting charge from periodicity ratios, though limitations regarding unique charge determination versus filling factor are noted.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail device schematics, SEM images, and maps showing $\delta R_t$ oscillations across various filling factors, including evidence for minimal excitation and doubled periodicity regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures quasiparticle charges and periodicities across different FQH states, first establishing the $e/4$ charge, then investigating the crossover behavior upon increasing coupling, and finally addressing measurement ambiguities like gate hysteresis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measuring the charge of the quasiparticles hosted by even-denominator fractional quantum Hall (FQH) states is essential to identify the topology of their ground state. Here, we use a gate-defined antidot in bilayer graphene, with an additional gate to control only the antidot potential, to measure the charge of the quasiparticles trapped around it in even-denominator FQH states. We observe a localized charge of $e/4$ at $ν=-5/2$, $-1/2$, and $3/2$, consistent with the minimal excitation expected for leading candidate even-denominator ground states, and $e/3$ at the hole-conjugate state $ν=2/3$. We further show that increasing the coupling between the antidot-bound states and extended edge states drives a crossover between two regimes, characterized by the minimal-excitation gate-voltage period and approximately twice that period, respectively. We discuss two possible explanations for this crossover: quasiparticle bunching and a crossover between distinct antidot transport regimes. Our results, together with previous observations of the daughter states, show that the even-denominator FQH states in bilayer graphene are compatible with a non-Abelian ground state, and that their quasiparticles can be localized around a quantum Hall antidot, a necessary ingredient for topological quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the significant experimental hurdle of performing Circular Dichroism spectroscopy on individual molecules. The authors develop a comprehensive theoretical and practical guide to systematically identify, quantify, and correct for polarization-related artifacts that plague single-object measurements. By achieving artifact suppression, the work enables the reliable probing of single-particle chirality in complex biophysical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it deals with the precise characterization and control of polarization states in complex, non-ideal optical systems, which is crucial for single-molecule spectroscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Performing Circular Dichroism (CD) spectroscopy on single molecular objects is extremely challenging because the lack of spatial and orientational averaging makes the measurements highly susceptible to experimental polarization artifacts.

**Main result.** The authors present a systematic theoretical and experimental framework demonstrating that reliable single-object CD measurements are achievable by rigorously characterizing and controlling polarization-related artifacts.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling (e.g., Taylor expansion of intensity distributions) with experimental implementation using fluorescence-excitation spectroscopy with controlled LCP/RCP light sources.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on single chiral molecular aggregates, such as chlorosomes, which are used as the physical system. The measurement relies on the difference in fluorescence-excitation spectra recorded using Left and Right Circularly Polarized light.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the dissymmetry factor, $g(\lambda_{35})$, which quantifies the chirality of the sample.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is concerned with controlling artifacts arising from LCP/RCP intensity imbalances, deviations from ideal circular polarization, and coupling with linear dichroism.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the use of small angle approximations for polarization variations and the ability to model complex polarization artifacts using Gaussian intensity distributions.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a schematic sketch of the experimental setup used for single-object CD spectroscopy, detailing the components for polarization control.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical basis of CD spectroscopy to detailing the experimental limitations imposed by single-object measurements, developing a mathematical framework to quantify polarization artifacts, and finally presenting the methodology for artifact suppression.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecular aggregates are at the basis of a broad range of functional systems, including photosynthetic light-harvesting complexes, organic photovoltaics, optoelectronic devices, and molecular sensors. Their structural organization, however, is often difficult to resolve because intrinsic heterogeneity. In this context, circular dichroism (CD) spectroscopy is particularly powerful because of its sensitivity to the three-dimensional organization of molecular building blocks. Extending CD measurements from ensembles to individual objects therefore offers a unique approach to probing supramolecular architecture in heterogeneous, non-crystallizable systems. However, without spatial and orientational averaging, measurements are highly sensitive to imbalances between left- and right-circularly polarized excitation, deviations from ideal circular polarization, and polarization imperfections coupled to linear dichroism, which creates spurious CD signals. Here, we present a systematic theoretical and experimental framework for understanding, quantifying, and controlling artifacts in single-object CD spectroscopy. We develop a mathematical description of the principal artifact mechanisms, identify their origins in practical implementations, including Pockels-cell-based polarization modulation, and establish strategies for their minimization. We validate this framework using an experimental implementation capable of acquiring CD spectra from individual molecular aggregates over 690-785 nm. Our results demonstrate that reliable single-object CD measurements are achievable when polarization-related artifacts are rigorously characterized and controlled. This Tutorial Review provides a unified theoretical framework and practical guide for single-object CD spectroscopy and its application to single-particle chirality, supramolecular photonics, and biophotonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work shows that an electrostatic spherical shell can host an exceptional point (EP) and exhibit non-Hermitian physics solely by exploiting dielectric anisotropy, without needing gain or loss. By mapping the radial problem to a damped oscillator, the authors demonstrate that crossing a hyperbolicity threshold causes the internal electric field to switch from decaying to oscillating. This establishes hyperbolic media as a powerful, passive platform for simulating non-Hermitian phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer non-Hermitian physics (like EPs) using purely classical, passive electromagnetic boundary conditions (hyperbolicity) is highly relevant for designing novel metamaterials and optical devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates that an exceptional point (EP) and non-Hermitian phase transition can be realized in a passive, reciprocal, and lossless electrostatic spherical shell using only dielectric anisotropy.

**Main result.** Hyperbolic media provide a platform to simulate non-Hermitian physics, causing the internal electric field to transition from monotonic decay to spatial oscillation across the EP.

**Method.** The problem is reduced to solving a Cauchy-Euler equation in the logarithmic radial coordinate, which maps onto a damped oscillator and a two-site Hatano-Nelson Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is an anisotropic core-shell nanoparticle where the permittivity tensor in the shell is diagonal, $\epsilon_b = 	ext{diag}(\epsilon_r, \epsilon_t, \epsilon_t)$. The physics is governed by the dielectric anisotropy factor, $\epsilon_t/\epsilon_r$.

**Key observables.** Electric field amplitude $|E|/E_0$, field localization in the $	heta$ parameter, and the appearance of anomalous secular modes at the EP.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dielectric anisotropy factor $\epsilon_t/\epsilon_r$ acts as the sole control parameter, defining regimes like elliptic, hyperbolic overdamped, and hyperbolic underdamped.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the dipolar term ($l=1$) in the initial notes, and the general framework uses separation of variables for the electrostatic potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup, the phase diagram of field exponents vs. anisotropy, and cross-sections of the field amplitude $|E|/E_0$ showing the transition across the EP for different anisotropy values.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the electrostatic potential using separation of variables, deriving a Cauchy-Euler equation. It then maps this equation to known non-Hermitian models (damped oscillator, Hatano-Nelson) to analyze the transition controlled by the anisotropy parameter.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional points (EPs) are non--Hermitian degeneracies at which both eigenvalues and eigenvectors coalesce, usually engineered through balanced gain and loss or non--reciprocity. In this work, we show that the radial electrostatic problem of an anisotropic core--shell nanoparticle realizes an EP without any of the alluded conditons. Written in the logarithmic radial coordinate, the problem is a Cauchy--Euler equation that maps onto a damped oscillator and, equivalently, onto a two-site Hatano--Nelson Hamiltonian, so that the non-Hermiticity is emulated in space rather than in time and the sole control parameter is the degree of dielectric anisotropy, $\varepsilon_t/\varepsilon_r$. The shell is passive, reciprocal, and lossless, yet it hosts an EP and a non--Hermitian phase transition governed by hyperbolicity alone. Beyond a hyperbolicity threshold the electric field inside the shell turns from monotonic decay into spatial oscillation, producing hotspots at intermediate radii which can be exploited to tailor fluorescence, strong coupling, sensing, or cloaking. More broadly, this establishes hyperbolic media as a platform to simulate non--Hermitian physics without gain, loss, or non--reciprocity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports an integrated diamond platform combining NV centers for magnetic sensing with BDD structures for microwave delivery and local thermometry. By demonstrating that the integrated BDD thermometer accurately measures local heating effects missed by external thermometers, the authors prove this platform is a practical tool for quantitative, wide-field magnetic imaging of temperature-sensitive materials like superconductors at cryogenic temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of an integrated, on-chip platform for quantum sensing that self-monitors its local thermal environment is crucial for quantitative studies of quantum materials under extreme conditions, bridging quantum sensing with advanced metrology.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Wide-field nitrogen-vacancy (NV) magnetic imaging at cryogenic temperatures requires both microwave excitation and accurate local temperature knowledge near the sensing region.

**Main result.** The integrated boron-doped diamond (BDD)–NV platform successfully enables wide-field magnetic imaging while simultaneously providing local thermometry, demonstrating its practicality for cryogenic quantum sensing.

**Method.** The authors integrated NV centers, a BDD microwave antenna, and a BDD thermometer onto a single diamond substrate to perform Optically Detected Magnetic Resonance (ODMR) measurements.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes NV centers in diamond for magnetic sensing, coupled with BDD structures for microwave delivery and local temperature monitoring. This integrated platform allows for simultaneous measurement of magnetic fields and thermal environment near superconducting samples.

**Key observables.** Temperature-dependent Meissner response of cuprate superconductors, magnetic-field maps, and local temperature readings (comparing BDD vs. stage thermometer).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperatures, microwave power, superconducting transition temperatures (Tc), and local heating effects (e.g., laser-induced heating > 4 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The BDD thermometer's resistance-to-temperature calibration required fitting data with a fourth-order polynomial, and the microwave power was estimated based on nominal input power.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the platform schematic, comparisons of temperature readings (BDD vs. stage thermometer) under varying power, and temperature-dependent wide-field ODMR imaging of superconducting samples.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for integrated sensing, details the platform construction (NV/BDD), presents comparative measurements showing the BDD thermometer's superior local temperature resolution, and concludes by applying the platform to map superconducting properties as a function of local temperature.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Wide-field nitrogen-vacancy (NV) magnetic imaging at cryogenic temperatures requires microwave excitation and reliable knowledge of the temperature near the sensing region. Here, we report an integrated diamond quantum-sensing platform combining an ensemble of NV centers with a boron-doped diamond (BDD) microwave antenna and thermometer formed on the same diamond substrate. The BDD antenna provides microwave excitation for optically detected magnetic resonance measurements, and the BDD thermometer monitors the thermal environment near the NV sensing region. The BDD thermometer detected laser-induced local heating that was not clearly resolved by a stage-mounted thermometer. Using this platform, we imaged the temperature-dependent Meissner response of multiple cuprate superconductors while recording the temperature. These results demonstrate that the integrated BDD--NV platform provides a practical approach for cryogenic wide-field magnetic imaging with integrated microwave delivery and local thermometry.

</details>
