**Summary.** This work presents a novel magnetic communication system pairing an acoustically actuated magnetoelectric resonator as a transmitter with a highly sensitive quantum diamond magnetometer receiver. By demonstrating amplitude and phase-encoded transmission at 20 kHz, the authors establish a viable, compact alternative to traditional loop-based magnetic communication methods for conductive environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics involves quantum sensing (NV centers), the application is in classical communication engineering. However, the use of quantum sensors to measure classical fields at low frequencies makes it relevant to open quantum systems research concerning environmental coupling and noise characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a compact and highly sensitive alternative magnetic communication system for short-to-medium range transmission in conductive media, overcoming limitations of traditional loop antennas.

**Main result.** The hybrid system combining an acoustically actuated magnetoelectric resonator transmitter and a quantum diamond magnetometer receiver successfully demonstrated communication, achieving sensitivities of 50 pT/sqrt(Hz) and 1.2 mrad/sqrt(Hz).

**Method.** The system uses amplitude and phase encoding of AC magnetic fields, measured via the spin-echo decay of Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, driven by an acoustically actuated magnetoelectric resonator.

**Model / system.** The platform consists of a transmitter using a multi-layered ME resonator heterostructure and a receiver utilizing a micrometer-scale NV diamond magnetometer. The magnetometry relies on measuring the phase accumulation ($\delta\phi$) via a Hahn-echo sequence.

**Key observables.** Amplitude and phase sensitivity (50 pT/sqrt(Hz) and 1.2 mrad/sqrt(Hz)), Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), and magnetic field decay with distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequency ($f_{AC} = 20 	ext{ kHz}$), spin coherence time ($T_2 = 231 \pm 3 \mu	ext{s}$), and field decay proportional to $1/d^3$ in the near-field.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation is residual laser-intensity noise, although the theoretical shot-noise limit is calculated to be $0.33 	ext{ pT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the hybrid system, the AC magnetometry measurement using Hahn-echo sequences, the successful transmission/reception using OOK and BPSK modulation, and the decay of the magnetic field strength with distance in various media.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup, demonstrates communication using modulation schemes (OOK/BPSK), analyzes the magnetic field decay in different media, and provides theoretical appendices on resonator characterization and noise limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Wireless communication via propagating magnetic fields is a communication modality that has recently garnered significant interest for short-to-medium range communication in conductive mediums, such as underwater and underground, where existing approaches utilizing electric fields are highly inefficient. Typical implementations of magnetic communication make use of loop antennas as both the transmitter and receiver, with the sensitivity and frequency response scaling with and inversely with the loop cross-sectional area, respectively. Here, we explore an alternative hybrid magnetic communication system consisting of an highly radiation efficient and compact acoustically actuated magnetoelectric resonator as the transmitter, and a highly sensitive micrometer scale quantum magnetometer based on nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond as the receiver, with their core properties unconstrained by size. We demonstrate amplitude and phase-encoded transmission and reception of AC magnetic fields at $f_{\mathrm{AC}}$ = 20 kHz, achieving a sensitivity of 50 pT/$\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ and 1.2 mrad/$\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$, respectively. This work establishes the use of hybrid magnetoelectric resonator and quantum diamond magnetometer communication system as a viable alternative to existing loop-based approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using the supercurrent flowing through Shiba-Majorana hybrid systems to detect topological phase transitions. By analyzing characteristic discontinuities in the critical supercurrent, the authors introduce a non-invasive method to read out and control the ground state parity of Majorana zero modes. This advances the field by linking measurable transport properties to abstract topological quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of supercurrent measurements as a diagnostic tool for topological transitions bridges superconducting transport measurements with fundamental topological physics, which is relevant for understanding quantum state readout in superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the detection and manipulation of topological phase transitions occurring in Shiba-Majorana hybrid superconducting systems.

**Main result.** Supercurrent discontinuities observed when passing supercurrent through the system provide a non-invasive method to read out and control the ground state parity of Majorana states.

**Method.** The authors utilize theoretical modeling based on the supercurrent calculation derived from the ground state energy, $I(\phi) = 2\partial E_{GS}(\phi) / \partial \phi$.

**Model / system.** The system involves Shiba-Majorana hybrid structures, modeled by an effective Hamiltonian $H_{eff}$ coupling a superconducting STM tip to low-energy Shiba-Majorana modes mediated by a control magnetic adatom.

**Key observables.** Supercurrent $I(\phi)$ as a function of superconducting phase bias $\phi$, and characteristic discontinuities in the critical current $I_c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting phase bias $\phi$, finite temperature $T$, and the relative energy scales of the Shiba and Majorana couplings ($|\epsilon_M|$ vs $|\epsilon_S|$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the system follows the lowest-energy branch and focuses on the contribution of discrete in-gap states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup involving adatom chains and the supercurrent jump coinciding with the sign change of the inner Majoranas hybridization energy, and show the effect of finite temperature smearing on the transition signature.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by projecting the Hamiltonian onto the low-energy subspace, deriving the supercurrent dependence on the phase bias, and then analyzing the resulting discontinuities as signatures of topological phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The non-Abelian statistics of Majorana zero modes has inspired numerous proposals for their detection and manipulation in topological superconductors. Implementations based on magnetic adatoms deposited on superconductors draw particular attention due to their capabilities for precise atomic manipulation and the control over disorder. Here, we propose a scheme for detecting changes in the ground state parity of a topologically non-trivial adatom system by passing supercurrent through their low-energy modes. We unravel characteristic discontinuities in the critical current driven by zero-energy level crossings. We apply these findings to a setup where the Majorana coupling is mediated by a single control magnetic adatom hosting a Yu-Shiba-Rusinov state, and test the robustness of our results against finite temperatures and different tunneling regimes. Our findings introduce a non-invasive approach for reading out and controlling the ground state parity of Majorana states in Shiba-Majorana hybrid systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that even when a magnet maintains a simple, collinear classical spin structure, quantum correlations can harbor hidden frustration. This frustration is induced by a symmetry-allowed Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, leading to topological vortices in the magnon correlations. These vortices manifest macroscopically as a pattern of counter-circulating, equilibrium spin currents in the quantum vacuum.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent, non-trivial quantum fluxes and currents existing entirely within the quantum vacuum, decoupled from static magnetic order, is highly relevant to understanding topological phases in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how hidden frustration, manifested as circulating quantum correlations, can exist in a magnet that remains classically collinear.

**Main result.** The system hosts a frustrated, current-carrying quantum vacuum despite classical collinearity, driven by symmetry-allowed Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya coupling.

**Method.** The analysis uses Holstein-Primakoff expansion, Bogoliubov diagonalization for magnon modes, and topological diagnostics like Berry curvature and Wilson loops.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on a 2D inverse Lieb lattice realized in a centrosymmetric altermagnet insulator, modeled by a spin Hamiltonian including exchange and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions.

**Key observables.** Anomalous magnon pair correlations, gauge-invariant fluxes, and antiferrochiral equilibrium spin currents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regime requires $J_1 > 0$, $D_1 
eq 0$, and $K > 0$ for the collinear G-type AFM ground state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed using linear spin-wave theory (Holstein-Primakoff expansion) and assumes a centrosymmetric structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the model lattice, the phase accumulation of the anomalous correlator $F_k$ showing vortices at high-symmetry points (X and Y), and vector plots of the Berry connection and curvature showing opposite-chirality vortices.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the Hamiltonian, linearizing it via Holstein-Primakoff, calculating magnon dispersions, identifying topological features (vortices) in the anomalous correlators, and finally deriving the real-space consequences as counter-circulating spin currents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a magnet can remain perfectly collinear while its quantum vacuum circulates. In a centrosymmetric altermagnet, a symmetry-allowed locally staggered Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya coupling imprints a gauge-irremovable vortex-antivortex pair into the anomalous magnon pair correlations at high-symmetry points in momentum space. In real space, these hidden vortices produce an antiferrochiral array of equilibrium spin currents circulating oppositely around neighboring plaquettes. Gauge-irremovable frustration therefore survives despite classical collinearity: it exists entirely in the quantum correlations. Our results show that complex pairing, gauge-invariant fluxes, and equilibrium loop currents - structures encountered across electronic flux phases, spin liquids, and frustrated quantum magnets - can be encoded in the squeezed vacuum of a collinear magnet.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using the coupling between magnons and phonons in a honeycomb phononic crystal to engineer a phononic Chern insulator. By breaking time-reversal symmetry via a circularly polarized magnon mode, the system opens a topological gap, allowing for the creation of tunable, non-dissipative edge states. This establishes a route for magnetically reconfigurable topological phononics in GHz acoustic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, solid-state platform (acoustic/phononic) for realizing topological phenomena, bridging concepts from magnetism (magnons) and condensed matter topology (Chern insulators) in a potentially observable, high-frequency domain.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to realize a phononic Chern insulator at high frequencies (GHz) to achieve robust, one-way boundary transport in acoustic devices.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that chiral magnon-phonon hybridization can create a topologically gapped phononic Chern phase with tunable Chern numbers (|C|=1 and |C|=2).

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective Hamiltonian using the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation in the large-detuning limit, leading to a Haldane-type model.

**Model / system.** The system is a honeycomb phononic crystal hybridized with ferromagnetic islands. The physics is governed by the coupling between lattice phonons and magnons (Kittel mode).

**Key observables.** Chern numbers (C), topological gaps ($\Delta_1, \Delta_2$), and the existence of chiral edge states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnon-phonon coupling strength ($g$), detuning ($\delta\omega$), and the ability to tune the Chern number by varying these parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-detuning approximation ($|\delta\omega| \gg g, t$) and assumes the coupling phase originates from projecting the right-circular Kittel mode.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the honeycomb structure, momentum-space coupling intensity, and bulk band structures showing the Chern numbers and the spatial distribution of edge states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the full Hamiltonian ($H = H_{ph} + H_m + H_{int}$), derives the effective Hamiltonian ($H_{eff}$) via Schrieffer-Wolff, and then analyzes the resulting Haldane-type model to predict tunable Chern phases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-frequency artificial phononic crystals offer a low-loss platform compatible with on-chip integration, yet realizing Chern phononic phases at GHz frequencies remains challenging. Here, we propose a magnon-induced phononic Chern insulator in a honeycomb phononic crystal hybridized with ferromagnetic islands at the hexagon centers. A circularly polarized Kittel mode couples to the surrounding phonons with a phase winding, which breaks time-reversal symmetry and opens a full Chern gap. In the large-detuning regime, this mechanism leads to an effective Haldane-type phononic model with magnon-induced complex hopping. By tuning the magnon-phonon interaction, the full hybrid system accesses Chern phases with tunable Chern numbers |C|=1 and |C|=2. The predicted gaps can exceed realistic phonon and magnon linewidths, enabling their observation in GHz acoustic devices. Our work establishes chiral magnon--phonon hybridization as a route to magnetically reconfigurable topological phononics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the observation of quantized Hall resistance in a supermoiré Chern mosaic formed from twisted graphene, contradicting initial expectations of gapless transport. The authors explain this robust quantization by proposing that a moderate magnetic field drives a valley-selective topological transition, establishing a global Chern insulator state with a Chern number of -6.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of field-induced topological phase transitions in moiré heterostructures, which can be modeled using continuum theories and analyzed via Chern numbers, provides a rich platform for understanding emergent topological quantum phenomena relevant to quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the mechanism behind quantized charge transport in a Supermoiré Chern Mosaic, contrasting the expected unquantized behavior from domain-wall modes with the observed quantized Hall resistance.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a robust, quantized Hall resistance ($R_{yx} \approx -h/6e^2$) under a perpendicular magnetic field, attributed to a field-induced, valley-selective topological transition that gaps the domain-wall modes.

**Method.** The research combines magnetotransport measurements (Landau fan measurements) with theoretical calculations using the Bistritzer-MacDonald continuum model and the Hofstadter spectrum analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is helical trilayer graphene (HTG) twisted by a magic angle ($\sim 1.8^\circ$), forming a supermoiré mosaic of domains with opposite valley-resolved Chern numbers.

**Key observables.** Quantized Hall resistance ($R_{yx}$), longitudinal resistance ($R_{xx}$), and the topological invariant, the Chern number ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (e.g., 4.6 K), perpendicular magnetic field ($B$), and the twist angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the orbital Zeeman model and assumes that the observed quantization implies a global gap, overriding the expected gapless domain-wall transport.

**Figures summary.** Figures show magnetotransport data (Landau Fans) confirming quantized plateaus, and theoretical figures illustrate the evolution of the Hofstadter spectrum and the conditions for a global Chern gap.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical system and the initial theoretical conflict (gapless vs. quantized transport) to presenting experimental measurements confirming quantization, and finally developing theoretical models (Hofstadter/Orbital Zeeman) to explain the field-induced topological transition responsible for the global gap.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magic-angle helical trilayer graphene---three graphene layers sequentially twisted in the same direction by $\sim1.8^\circ$---relaxes into a mosaic of domains that, at zero field, carry opposite valley-resolved Chern numbers, with boundaries hosting a network of gapless conducting modes. Charge transport through this network depends sensitively on how the modes connect and scatter, making well-quantized transport unlikely. Contrary to this expectation, we observe a field-induced Chern gap with Chern number $C=-6$ emanating from charge neutrality; in this gap, the Hall resistance is quantized to within $2\%$ of the expected value, $-h/6e^2$, at 4.6 K. We explain this behavior using both Hofstadter and orbital Zeeman calculations, which show that a moderate magnetic field drives a valley-selective topological transition. Above the transition, the total Chern number of the occupied states in each spin-valley flavor becomes identical across neighboring domains, and the domain-wall modes can become gapped. Though the central valence-band Chern numbers still differ between the two domain types, the observed quantized transport attests to a global gap.

</details>

**Authors:** Marco Calabrese, Gino Biondini, Christopher Chong, Panayotis Kevrekidis

**Summary.** This paper analyzes dispersive shock wave formation in the 2D Toda lattice using wedge-type initial conditions. The key finding is that the interaction of these waves exhibits a discrete Mach reflection, characterized by a critical slope that separates resonant (expanding) from ordinary (localized peak) interaction regimes. The work provides a rigorous analytical framework validated against numerical simulations and the KP equation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is in nonlinear wave dynamics, the mathematical techniques—such as the use of integrable systems (Toda lattice) and modulation theory—are highly relevant to understanding complex, non-equilibrium dynamics in many-body quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation and interaction of Dispersive Shock Waves (DSWs) within the framework of the two-dimensional Toda lattice (2DTL) when subjected to wedge-type initial conditions.

**Main result.** The interaction of the generated oblique DSWs yields a discrete analog of Mach reflection, with the dynamics bifurcating into resonant (expanding DSW) and ordinary (localized peak) regimes depending on a critical slope parameter $q_{cr}$.

**Method.** The analysis uses local reduction to the one-dimensional Toda lattice (Riemann problem) and employs Whitham modulation theory to determine soliton amplitudes, validating results against numerical simulations and the continuum KP equation limit.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the two-dimensional Toda lattice (2DTL), described by a specific non-linear partial difference equation. The system is initialized with wedge-type conditions defined by a slope parameter $q$.

**Key observables.** The amplitude, length, and velocity of the resulting DSWs, and the existence of a critical slope $q_{cr}$ separating dynamic regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The slope parameter $q$ defining the wedge geometry, and the critical slope $q_{cr}$ which dictates the nature of the wave interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of Whitham modulation theory and relies on the consistency between the discrete 2DTL results and the continuum limit theory for the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili (KP) equation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometrically distinct initial data for Type I and Type II wedges. Later figures show density plots illustrating the formation of tilted stems in asymmetric subcritical/supercritical regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the 1D Toda lattice Riemann problem to the 2D DSW interaction in the 2DTL. It classifies dynamics based on wedge type (I/II) and parameter regimes (sub/supercritical), deriving explicit analytical solutions for the resulting wave structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the formation and interaction of dispersive shock waves (DSWs) in the two-dimensional Toda lattice subject to wedge-type initial conditions, and show that their interaction gives rise to a discrete analog of Mach reflection for dispersive shock waves in discrete systems. The initial jump across each leg of the wedge acts locally as a Riemann problem for the one-dimensional Toda lattice, producing two oblique DSWs whose leading-edge soliton amplitude is determined explicitly by the one-dimensional Whitham modulation theory. The two-dimensional nature of the problem manifests when these oblique DSWs meet along the symmetry axis. We show that, for compressive wedges (i.e., when the initial conditions are such that two oblique DSWs that are generated propagate toward each other), a critical slope $q_{\mathrm{cr}}$ separates two regimes: in the subcritical regime ($q<q_{\mathrm{cr}}$) the interaction is resonant and it produces an expanding DSW whose amplitude, length and velocity are explicitly computed by using exact soliton solutions of the two-dimensional Toda lattice; in the supercritical regime ($q>q_{\mathrm{cr}}$) the interaction is ordinary and produces a localized peak whose amplitude is determined analytically. We also show qualitatively that a similar dichotomy between two regimes exists for expansive wedges (i.e., when the initial conditions are such that the two oblique DSWs propagate away from each other). We confirm all analytical predictions by comparing them with the results of direct numerical simulations. Finally, we show that the continuum limit of the result is consistent with the analogous theory for the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili equation, providing an independent validation of the analytical framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically analyzes the Terahertz anomalous Hall effect in the Weyl semimetal $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$. By developing an effective low-energy model Hamiltonian, the authors calculate the intrinsic optical conductivity components ($\sigma_{xx}$ and $\sigma_{xy}$). The theoretical results provide a robust explanation for the observed magneto-optical behavior by linking it directly to the momentum-space separation of the Weyl nodes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter physics, the use of linear response theory (Kubo formalism) to model optical response and the explicit connection between band topology (Weyl nodes) and macroscopic transport properties (AHE) are relevant concepts in open quantum systems and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Terahertz anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in the magnetic Weyl semimetal $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$ by linking its intrinsic optical response to the momentum-space separation of its Weyl nodes.

**Main result.** The theoretical model successfully reproduces the measured magneto-optical phenomena, explaining the longitudinal and transverse conductivity signatures in the THz range via intrinsic gyrotropy.

**Method.** The authors employ the Kubo formalism to calculate optical conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}, \sigma_{xy}$) using a minimal two-band effective Hamiltonian, comparing these calculations against THz time-domain spectroscopy (THz-TDS) measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is the magnetic Weyl semimetal $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}_2	ext{S}_2$. The analysis uses a minimal two-band model Hamiltonian describing the electronic structure near the Weyl nodes, which exhibit Berry curvature sources and sinks.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal conductivity ($	ext{Re}[\sigma_{xx}]$), transverse (Hall) conductivity ($	ext{Re}[\sigma_{xy}]$), and the Faraday rotation angle ($	ext{Re}[\Theta_F]$) in the THz frequency range.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Momentum-space separation ($Q$) between Weyl nodes, chemical potential ($\mu$), and the energy parameter $b$ derived from the material structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include neglecting interband transitions ($\hbar\omega \ll \mu$) and assuming an effective low-energy model based on the two-node picture.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare measured $	ext{Re}[\sigma_{xx}]$ vs. temperature with model calculations, show $	ext{Re}[\sigma_{xy}]$ saturation, and illustrate the temperature dependence of the chemical potential.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical basis of AHE in Weyl semimetals, develops a minimal effective Hamiltonian, calculates the optical conductivity components ($\sigma_{xx}, \sigma_{xy}$) using the Kubo formula, and finally compares these theoretical predictions with experimental THz-TDS data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Time-reversal-symmetry-broken Weyl semimetals are known to have at least two nodes in their electronic band structure, separated in momentum space and acting as sources and sinks of Berry curvature. This gives rise to a transverse Hall conductivity, known as the anomalous Hall effect (AHE), which, in the simplest two-node picture, is proportional to the momentum-space separation between the nodes in the zero frequency limit. In the recently discovered Weyl semimetal $\mathrm{Co_3Sn_2S_2}$, a giant AHE has been observed. However, experimental investigations in the low-energy regime, which directly probe quasiparticle excitations near the Weyl nodes, remain limited. Here, we present a systematic study of the intrinsic low-energy gyrotropic optical response of $\mathrm{Co_3Sn_2S_2}$ using terahertz spectroscopy combined with semianalytical calculations based on a physically intuitive effective model. Our results provide a robust and transparent explanation of the observed magnetooptical phenomena in terms of intrinsic gyrotropy arising from momentum-space separation of the Weyl nodes. Furthermore, quantitative comparison between experiment and theory places stringent constraints on the material parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the synthesis and characterization of $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}$, a room-temperature kagome ferromagnet thin film. Experimentally, the material exhibits a large, temperature-independent anomalous Hall effect. Theoretically, the AHE is successfully linked to intrinsic Berry curvature hotspots calculated from the material's spin-split band structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly related to quantum information, the study of topological electronic properties (Berry curvature) in magnetic materials is foundational to understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter physics, which underpins quantum computing hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the intrinsic anomalous Hall response in the bilayer kagome ferromagnet $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}$.

**Main result.** The material exhibits robust easy-plane ferromagnetism and a large, temperature-independent anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{	ext{AHE}} \approx 90 \ \Omega^{-1} \cdot 	ext{cm}^{-1}$), which is theoretically attributed to intrinsic Berry curvature hotspots.

**Method.** The research combines experimental techniques like Molecular Beam Epitaxy (MBE) and magneto-transport measurements with first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is high-quality thin films of $	ext{Co}_3	ext{Sn}$, a kagome metal, grown epitaxially in a bilayer stacking sequence. The electronic properties are analyzed using DFT to map the spin-split band structure and Berry curvature.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{	ext{AHE}}$), magnetic anisotropy (easy-plane ferromagnetism), and the Berry curvature hotspots near the Fermi level.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Curie temperature ($T_C > 300\,	ext{K}$), carrier density ($n\approx5.01	imes10^{22}\,	ext{cm}^{-3}$), and the temperature independence of $\sigma_{	ext{AHE}}$ up to room temperature.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalous Hall response is assumed to be dominated by the intrinsic Berry curvature effect, which is confirmed by matching DFT calculations to experimental transport data.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the kagome structure, characterization results (XRD, XPS, $M(H)$ isotherms showing easy-plane ferromagnetism), and transport measurements showing the temperature-independent AHE.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard structure: synthesis and structural characterization (MBE, XRD, XPS) $ightarrow$ magnetic characterization (Magnetometry) $ightarrow$ transport measurements (Hall effect, AHE) $ightarrow$ theoretical modeling (DFT calculation of Berry curvature) $ightarrow$ conclusion linking theory and experiment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transition-metal kagome magnets provide a rich platform for investigating the interplay between layer stacking, magnetic order, and band topology. Here, we report the molecular beam epitaxy and experimental investigation of high-quality thin films of the kagome metal Co$_3$Sn, which has not been synthesized in bulk form yet. Structural and chemical analyses confirm a hexagonal lattice structure ($P6_3/mmc$) composed of direct A-B stacked Co$_3$Sn kagome bilayers. Magnetometry reveals robust easy-plane ferromagnetism with a Curie temperature exceeding $300\,\text{K}$. Magneto-transport measurements demonstrate metallic behavior (carrier density $n\approx5.01\times10^{22}\,\text{cm}^{-3}$) alongside a temperature-independent anomalous Hall conductivity of $σ_{\rm AHE}\approx90\,Ω^{-1}\cdot{\rm cm}^{-1}$, extending from $2\,\text{K}$ up to room temperature. Results from first-principles density functional theory calculations attribute this anomalous Hall response to intrinsic Berry curvature hotspots near the Fermi level in the spin-split band structure. Our results establish Co$_3$Sn as a room-temperature kagome ferromagnet and highlight the impact of the layer stacking sequence on the material properties of kagome metals from the CoSn family.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a straightforward method—inserting a buried Si layer—to significantly reduce detrimental oxygen impurities in Si/SiGe quantum wells. By correlating impurity reduction with transport measurements, the authors show that while oxygen is a key mobility limiter, the fundamental density-dependent transport physics remains unchanged, providing a crucial materials engineering handle for improving quantum device performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on semiconductor device physics, the rigorous characterization of disorder effects ($\alpha(n)$ vs. $n_p$) and the engineering solution for impurity mitigation are highly relevant to controlling disorder in solid-state quantum platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is reducing disorder, specifically oxygen impurities, in undoped Si/SiGe heterostructures, which limits performance in scalable quantum devices like electron spin qubits.

**Main result.** Introducing a thin, buried Si layer successfully reduces the oxygen background concentration by a factor of five, leading to an increased absolute electron mobility without altering the fundamental density-dependent transport scaling or percolation threshold.

**Method.** The study combines materials characterization using Secondary-ion Mass Spectrometry (SIMS) with detailed magnetotransport measurements (Hall-bar FETs) across varying carrier densities and temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an undoped Si/SiGe quantum-well heterostructure grown via RP-CVD on 200 mm Si(100) substrates. The key modification is the introduction of an electrically passive buried Si layer within the SiGe barrier.

**Key observables.** Electron mobility ($\mu$), oxygen background concentration (SIMS), percolation density ($n_p$), and the density-dependent mobility exponent ($\alpha(n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (0.3 K to 1.5 K), carrier density ($n$), and the concentration of oxygen impurities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The buried Si layer is assumed to be electrically passive and acts solely as a physical barrier to trap oxygen impurities during growth.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare SIMS depth profiles showing oxygen reduction; transport measurements compare $\mu(n)$, $n_p$, and $\alpha(n)$ curves for high- vs. low-oxygen devices at different temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of oxygen-limited mobility. It then details the engineering solution (buried Si layer) and uses SIMS to confirm oxygen reduction. Finally, it presents magnetotransport data showing that while absolute mobility increases, the characteristic density-dependent transport scaling remains robust.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reducing disorder in undoped Si/SiGe field-effect heterostructures remains an important materials challenge for scalable quantum devices, particularly electron spin qubits. Background impurities such as oxygen have been identified as mobility-limiting, yet practical heterostructure-design strategies for suppressing their incorporation remain underexplored, and their influence across different transport regimes is not fully established. Here, we demonstrate a simple route to oxygen reduction and mobility enhancement in Si/SiGe quantum-well heterostructures grown by reduced-pressure chemical vapor deposition (RP-CVD) on 200 mm Si(100) substrates through the introduction of a thin, electrically passive buried Si layer within the lower SiGe barrier. Secondary-ion mass spectrometry shows that the buried Si layer reproducibly reduces the oxygen background in the subsequently grown SiGe by approximately a factor of five, without modifying the active quantum-well region. Density- and temperature-dependent magnetotransport measurements further show that this reduction increases the electron mobility, while leaving the percolation density and density-dependent mobility scaling largely unchanged. Upon cooling to 0.3 K, both high- and low-oxygen devices exhibit similar density-dependent fractional mobility enhancements, indicating that the reduced oxygen background improves momentum relaxation without substantially altering the dominant low-density disorder landscape. These results establish the buried Si layer as a straightforward and process-compatible heterostructure-design element for reducing oxygen incorporation and improving transport in 200 mm CVD-grown Si/SiGe quantum-device materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on achieving a giant bulk photovoltaic effect in a $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{Ta}_2	ext{NiSe}_5$ heterostructure by engineering interfacial symmetry breaking. Using a specialized cross-bar device, the authors successfully separated competing photophysical mechanisms to quantify a high BPV response. This work provides a platform for designing self-powered optoelectronic devices based on interface engineering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state optoelectronics, the concept of engineered symmetry breaking to control electronic response is analogous to controlling coupling terms in open quantum systems or designing tailored quantum interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is disentangling the microscopic origins and optimizing the bulk photovoltaic (BPV) effect in complex Van der Waals (vdW) heterostructures where multiple photophysical mechanisms overlap.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate a giant BPV effect localized at the $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{Ta}_2	ext{NiSe}_5$ interface, achieving a high photocurrent density ($247 	ext{ A/cm}^2$) by exploiting symmetry breaking.

**Method.** The study combines advanced electrical measurements (I-V, photovoltage mapping, gate-tuning) with theoretical modeling (DFT calculations of conductivity tensors).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{Ta}_2	ext{NiSe}_5$ vdW heterostructure, studied using a cross-bar device geometry to isolate interfacial photoresponse.

**Key observables.** Zero-bias photocurrent density ($247 	ext{ A/cm}^2$), BPV coefficient ($0.99 	ext{ V}^{-1}$), localized photovoltage enhancement, and gate-tunable barrier heights.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage ($	ext{V}_{	ext{BG}}$), vertical electric field ($	ext{V}_z$), and laser power density ($P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The cross-bar geometry is crucial as a strategy to spatially separate and independently probe the contributions of interfacial symmetry breaking versus intralayer effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device architecture, the localization of the BPV effect at the overlap region, the dependence of photoresponse on external biases, and comparisons of the BPV coefficient against other materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the complex nature of BPV in vdW heterostructures, presenting the cross-bar device setup for controlled measurement, quantifying the giant BPV effect through electrical characterization, and supporting these findings with theoretical calculations of symmetry-dependent charge transport.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Van der Waals (vdW) heterostructures offer a versatile platform for engineering unconventional bulk photovoltaic (BPV) effect through interfacial symmetry breaking. However, the coexistence of multiple photophysical mechanisms, driven by structural complexity, spontaneous charge transfer, and strong interlayer coupling, often obscures the microscopic origin of the BPV response and hinders its rational optimization. Here, we demonstrate a pronounced BPV effect localized at the overlap region of a cross-bar MoS2/Ta2NiSe5 vdW heterostructure, where symmetry breaking induced by vertical stacking lifts the inversion center of MoS2. The orthogonal device geometry enables the independent probing of intralayer and interfacial photoresponse pathways, facilitating clear separation of competing mechanisms. Spontaneous interfacial charge transfer between MoS2 and Ta2NiSe5 further establishes a strong interlayer electronic coupling. By modulating the interlayer potential landscape through gate voltage and vertical electric fields, we achieve an optimized zero-bias photocurrent density of 247 A/cm2 and a BPV coefficient of 0.99 V-1. Supported by theoretical modelling, our results illustrate how minimalist device geometry can transform complex heterostructures into experimentally tractable platforms. This strategy paves the way for analyzing and optimizing interface-driven BPV effect, with implications for self-powered optoelectronics, broadband photodetection, and energy-harvesting nanodevices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the direct observation of dodecagonal replica bands in $30^\circ$-twisted bilayer $	ext{WSe}_2$ using ARPES. By analyzing the spectral reconstruction, the authors confirm the presence of a van Hove singularity near the K-valleys. This suggests that large-angle twisted bilayer TMDs are promising platforms for inducing novel electronic states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study investigates emergent electronic properties (like vHs) in engineered van der Waals heterostructures using advanced spectroscopic techniques, which is highly relevant to understanding correlated phases in low-dimensional quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to determine the electronic structure and scattering processes within 2D quasicrystals exhibiting multiorbital dodecagonal replica bands.

**Main result.** The authors directly observed replica bands in $30^\circ$-twisted bilayer $	ext{WSe}_2$ using ARPES, attributing them to interlayer Umklapp scattering and confirming a van Hove singularity near the K-valleys.

**Method.** The study combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements with theoretical modeling using a two-band model to simulate the observed spectral function.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $30^\circ$-twisted bilayer $	ext{WSe}_2$, which forms a quasicrystal structure. The analysis employs a two-band model to describe the hybridization and spectral reconstruction caused by interlayer coupling between the top and bottom layers.

**Key observables.** Direct observation of dodecagonal replica bands, spectral function measurements showing a van Hove singularity (vHs) near the K-valleys, and layer-dependent intensity asymmetries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The twist angle of $30^\circ$, the energy of the vHs ($-1.05 	ext{ eV}$), and the replica band maximum energy ($-0.86 	ext{ eV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that only states from the top and bottom valence bands mix, and that scattering processes can be modeled using first-order interlayer coupling approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ARPES setup and spectra along $\Gamma-	ext{K}$ cuts, showing replica bands. Other figures compare measured intensity to predictions from the two-band model, highlighting the vHs location and the effect of gap/linewidth on the spectral shape.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical system and the unknown electronic structure to presenting ARPES measurements confirming replica bands. It then develops a theoretical framework (two-band model) to explain the observed spectral weight transfer and the existence of the vHs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted bilayers of two-dimensional (2D) transition metal dichalcogenides are promising systems for achieving tunable quasicrystalline orders with emergent properties. The underpinning electronic structure and scattering processes in 2D quasicrystals with multiorbital dodecagonal replica bands have so far not been determined. Here, we utilize angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) with micrometer spatial resolution to directly observe replica bands in 30$^{\circ}$-twisted bilayer WSe$_2$. The symmetry and intensity distribution of the observed replicas are explained by interlayer Umklapp scattering from bottom to top WSe$_2$ layers. Our spectral function measurements are consistent with the presence of a van Hove singularity adjacent to the $\mathrm{K}$-valleys, which underlines the possibility of inducing electronic reconstructions in bilayers with a large interlayer twist angle.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work models the magnetotransport of 2D electron fluids in samples with rough edges by treating the system hydrodynamically. It derives expressions for the Hall resistance correction, $\deltaho_{xy}(B)$, which is predicted to show a characteristic minimum at high magnetic fields when edge roughness is significant. This minimum provides a measurable signature to experimentally determine the degree of edge roughness.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the use of hydrodynamic equations and the development of diagnostic signatures (like the minimum in $\deltaho_{xy}$) to characterize boundary conditions is relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory to describe the hydrodynamic magnetotransport of two-dimensional (2D) electron fluids in samples whose edges possess arbitrary roughness.

**Main result.** The Hall resistance correction, $\deltaho_{xy}(B)$, exhibits a non-monotonic dependence on the magnetic field, showing a minimum at sufficiently rough edges, which can be used as a diagnostic tool to measure edge roughness.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Navier-Stokes equations for the electron fluid flow, incorporating boundary conditions derived from a local collision operator to model edge scattering.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D electron gas confined in a sample geometry, modeled as a viscous electron fluid. The theory accounts for bulk scattering (defects) and boundary scattering (edges) using a hydrodynamic approximation for the distribution function.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal and Hall resistances ($ho_{xx}, ho_{xy}$), and specifically the relaxation contribution to the Hall resistance, $\deltaho_{xy}(B)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Edge roughness parameter ($a$), sample width ($W$), bulk defect scattering rate ($\gamma_{imp}$), and the magnetic field ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on the hydrodynamic approximation for the distribution function and assumes the electron-electron collision time is much shorter than the impurity scattering time ($	au_{ee} \ll 	au_{imp}$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate velocity profiles, longitudinal resistivity, and the Hall correction ($\deltaho_{xy}$) for varying sample widths and edge roughness parameters, showing the minimum dependence on the magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the theory by defining the governing equations (Navier-Stokes, collision integrals) for the hydrodynamic flow. It then calculates the resistivities ($ho_{xx}, ho_{xy}$) and analyzes the magnetic field dependence of the Hall correction ($\deltaho_{xy}$), establishing diagnostic criteria based on the minimum value.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In ultra-clean conductors, fast inter-particle collisions can lead to the formation of a viscous electron fluid and realization of the hydrodynamic transport regime. Here we develop a theory of hydrodynamic magnetotransport of two-dimensional (2D) electrons in samples with low densities of defects and edges of arbitrary roughness. Within our model the roughness is described by a single parameter with the dimension of speed in the boundary conditions on sample edges. The electron-fluid flow profiles in long samples, as well as the corresponding longitudinal and Hall resistances, are calculated. The contribution to the Hall resistance associated with the relaxation processes exhibits a saturation in the limit of high magnetic field and a minimum as a function of the magnetic field for sufficiently rough edges. The minimum disappears as the edge roughness decreases or the sample width and bulk scattering by defects increase. These properties of the Hall resistance can serve as the signs to identify the hydrodynamic regime of electron transport in experiments and can be used to determine its parameters, in particular, the degree of edge roughness.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates how sub-terahertz optical spectroscopy can be used to selectively probe the edge states in Fractional Quantum Hall systems. By employing the Composite Fermion mean-field theory, the authors calculate characteristic absorption peaks whose frequency scale directly reveals the electron-electron interaction-induced effective mass. This offers a powerful, non-invasive optical diagnostic tool for understanding strongly correlated quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable optical signature (sub-THz absorption) linked directly to the interaction physics ($m_{CF}^*$) governing strongly correlated electron systems, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to selectively probe and excite the edge states present in Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) effect samples using sub-terahertz optical spectroscopy.

**Main result.** The calculated edge-state absorption peaks appear in the millimeter-wave to sub-terahertz range, providing a direct optical probe of the electron-electron interaction-induced effective mass ($m_{CF}^*$).

**Method.** The authors calculated the absorbance spectrum using the Composite Fermion (CF) mean-field framework, analyzing the difference between $\hat{y}$- and $\hat{x}$-polarized absorption to isolate edge contributions.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D electron gas in the FQH regime, modeled by mapping the strongly correlated problem onto an effective integer quantum Hall problem using Composite Fermions. The analysis involves solving a differential equation derived from the CF Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** CF edge-state absorption peaks (60–500 GHz at 10 T), the number of resolved peaks (counting filled $\Lambda$-levels), and the difference spectrum $A^{\hat{y}} - A^{\hat{x}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions ($
u = p/(2p+1)$), magnetic field ($B=10$ T), and the CF effective mass ($m_{CF}^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations are performed within the CF mean-field approximation, and the edge state contribution is isolated by calculating the difference spectrum $A^{\hat{y}} - A^{\hat{x}}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the CF $\Lambda$-level dispersion and transition energies near a hard-wall edge, and show the calculated dimensionless absorbance difference $A^{\hat{y}} - A^{\hat{x}}$ for various filling fractions ($
u = 1/3, 2/5, 3/7$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (probing FQH edge states), details the theoretical framework (CF mean-field mapping), outlines the calculation of optical response using polarization differences, and presents the key results showing spectral fingerprints and mass determination.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that edge states in fractional quantum Hall effect samples can be selectively probed and excited with sub-terahertz optical spectroscopy. Using the composite fermion (CF) mean-field framework, which maps the strongly correlated fractional quantum Hall problem onto an effective integer quantum Hall problem, we calculate the absorbance spectrum for the Jain sequence of filling fractions including both bulk and edge states. The CF edge-state absorption peaks appear in the millimeter-wave to sub-terahertz range, e.g., 60-500 GHz at B = 10 T in GaAs, i.e. they are blueshifted with respect to the bulk CF cyclotron frequency but are well below the integer quantum Hall cyclotron frequency scale at the same magnetic fields. The number of resolved peaks in each series of the absorption spectrum counts the filled Lambda-levels and fingerprints the fraction. Inversion symmetry breaking near the edge activates optical transitions forbidden in the bulk and enables second-order nonlinear processes in electric-dipole approximation. The absolute frequency scale of the spectrum is set by the CF effective mass, which is generated entirely by electron-electron interactions, so the absorption spectrum provides a direct optical probe of this interaction-induced mass.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an iterative multiphysics model to study thermal runaway in VO2 nanostructures undergoing a phase transition. By coupling electrodynamics and heat transfer, the authors predict that the runaway threshold is highly sensitive to external factors like ambient temperature and substrate properties. This provides crucial design principles for active photonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong, non-linear, and temperature-dependent nature of the optical absorption coefficient ($\sigma_{	ext{abs}}(T)$) driving the thermal runaway mechanism shares conceptual links with non-equilibrium phase transitions studied in statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates optically induced thermal runaway in phase-change VO2 nanostructures, addressing the failure of existing models near sharp optical transitions.

**Main result.** Thermal runaway is predicted, showing that the ignition threshold depends on wavelength, ambient temperature, and substrate; preheating significantly lowers the required optical intensity.

**Method.** An iterative multiphysics framework is introduced, coupling full-wave electrodynamics with steady-state heat transfer calculations.

**Model / system.** The system involves VO2 nanostructures exhibiting an insulator-metal transition (IMT) and strong temperature-dependent optical properties. The model iteratively solves for the temperature change ($\Delta T$) by coupling absorbed power ($\dot{Q}$) to heat transfer.

**Key observables.** Thermal runaway behavior, required optical intensity for ignition (ITR), and steady-state temperature maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Illumination wavelength ($\lambda_{	ext{exc}}$), incident intensity ($I_{	ext{inc}}$), ambient temperature ($T_0$), and substrate thermal conductivity ($\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model requires iterative calculations because the change in temperature significantly alters the material's dielectric function, invalidating one-step approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures show parametric maps of steady-state temperature dependence on wavelength and intensity, confirming step-like runaway signatures. Other figures compare heating on different substrates, highlighting the dominant role of substrate thermal conductivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the multiphysics framework, demonstrates the runaway effect in free-standing structures, and then systematically analyzes how the surrounding substrate (via $\kappa$ and $\epsilon$) modifies the thermal runaway threshold and steady-state temperature profiles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While thermal runaway occurs across diverse physical disciplines, its presence in subwavelength phase-change photonics remains unexplored because existing thermo-optical models break down near sharp optical transitions. Here, we introduce an iterative multiphysics framework coupling full-wave electrodynamics with heat transfer, and we discover optically induced thermal runaway in vanadium dioxide (VO2) nanostructures. The runaway switching threshold depends on the illumination wavelength, ambient temperature, and underlying substrate, with external thermal biasing significantly reducing the optical intensity required for runaway ignition. Our results push forward the modeling of sharp photothermal transitions and provide essential design principles for active metasurfaces, neuromorphic photonic devices, and nanoscale thermal management.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces e-beam evaporated aluminum oxide (AlOx) as a superior, scalable encapsulation method for highly air-sensitive 2D quantum materials. By demonstrating that AlOx preserves the electronic and optical properties of materials like WTe2 and superconducting FeTeSe, the authors resolve a major bottleneck in fabricating functional quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily materials science, the ability to precisely control the environment and interface chemistry of quantum materials using a scalable deposition technique is crucial for realizing any quantum device, linking directly to open quantum system engineering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Air-sensitive 2D materials are difficult to integrate into functional devices due to environmental degradation and complex fabrication requirements.

**Main result.** Electron-beam evaporated aluminum oxide (AlOx) serves as a scalable, simple encapsulation layer that preserves the intrinsic electronic and optical properties of various 2D materials, enabling straightforward device fabrication.

**Method.** The authors used e-beam evaporation to deposit AlOx over 2D materials and characterized the results using Raman spectroscopy, s-SNOM, and electrical transport measurements.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on several 2D materials, including rare-earth tritellurides (RTe3), semimetallic WTe2, and superconducting iron-based compounds (FeTe_xSe_{1-x}). These materials are tested both in their pristine state and after encapsulation.

**Key observables.** Oxidation peaks (TeOx) in Raman spectra, plasmonic response contrast via s-SNOM, superconducting critical temperature (Tc) from R vs T measurements, and preservation of intrinsic optical/electronic properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** AlOx thickness (e.g., 3 nm, 130 nm), flake thickness, temperature range (low T for superconductivity), and substrate type (SiO2 vs. Al2O3).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method overcomes the traditional trade-off between effective encapsulation and simple, scalable device fabrication procedures.

**Figures summary.** Figures show Raman spectra comparing encapsulated vs. uncapped flakes (demonstrating oxidation suppression), s-SNOM images showing preserved optical contrast through AlOx, and R vs T curves confirming superconductivity in encapsulated devices.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically demonstrates the protective capability of AlOx using RTe3 (Raman analysis), then validates its compatibility with optical probing (s-SNOM on WTe2), and finally proves its utility in device fabrication by maintaining superconductivity in FeTeSe.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Air-sensitive 2D materials present a fundamental challenge for device integration. Encapsulation is often required to preserve intrinsic properties, yet conventional protection strategies often fail for thicker layers and complicate fabrication. Here, we demonstrate that electron-beam (e-beam) evaporated aluminum oxide ($\mathrm{AlO}_x$) serves as both an effective encapsulation layer and a platform for direct device fabrication. Unlike transfer-based approaches, this scalable method is compatible with thicker flakes and full device or wafer coverage. It requires no stacking procedures and enables contacts without post-encapsulation etching. Using rare-earth tritellurides ($\mathrm{RTe}_3$, R = La, Er), semimetallic $\mathrm{WTe}_2$, and superconducting $\mathrm{FeTe}_x\mathrm{Se}_{1-x}$, we show that $\mathrm{AlO}_x$ suppresses oxidation and preserves intrinsic optical and electronic properties. We establish substrate-dependent optimization of encapsulation across a range of flake thicknesses, demonstrate that ultrathin $\mathrm{AlO}_x$ preserves $\mathrm{WTe}_2$'s plasmonic response and maintains superconducting performance in $\mathrm{FeTe}_x\mathrm{Se}_{1-x}$. Thus we overcome the longstanding tradeoff between encapsulation and straightforward device fabrication in fragile quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates that molecules possessing discrete screw symmetry can dramatically enhance spin preservation during electron transport. This effect, which surpasses predictions based solely on spin-orbit coupling, is quantified by calculating spin fidelity and predicted to manifest as a strong, measurable magnetoresistance in a spin-valve device.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses spin-dependent transport in molecular systems, linking topological/geometric symmetries (screw symmetry) to robust quantum phenomena (spin preservation), which is highly relevant to molecular electronics and quantum spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how spin polarization is preserved during electronic transport through long molecular structures, particularly focusing on the role of discrete screw symmetry.

**Main result.** Screw symmetry significantly enhances spin preservation far beyond the timescale predicted by spin-orbit coupling, leading to a measurable, symmetry-protected magnetoresistance signature in a spin-valve setup.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Bloch Hamiltonian formalism, analyzing spin-flip probabilities via perturbation theory and calculating spin fidelity over time.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using tight-binding Hamiltonians for molecules exhibiting discrete screw symmetry. The analysis is extended to a spin-valve setup connecting the molecule to two magnetic leads.

**Key observables.** Spin fidelity (F), zero-bias conductance (G(V=0)), and the ratio of currents in different magnetic alignments (e.g., $	ext{Tr}/	ext{Lg}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chirality ($\chi$), pitch ($c$), screw angle ($	heta$), and the degree of symmetry breaking ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the spin-splitting energy remains small compared to other energy scales, and that phonon scattering is negligible compared to hopping amplitudes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spin-dependent band shift ($\Delta E_k$) due to screw geometry, the decay of spin fidelity as symmetry is broken, and the resulting spin-valve current-voltage characteristics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using the screw symmetry operator and Bloch Hamiltonian, to testing the effect using tight-binding models, and finally demonstrating the physical consequence via transport simulations in a spin-valve setup.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In electronic transport through long molecules, spin is expected to be preserved only when the dwell time is much shorter than the characteristic spin-mixing timescale $\hbar/Δ$, where $Δ$ is the magnitude of a spin-dependent potential, such as spin-orbit coupling. We show that, in molecules featuring discrete screw symmetry, spin preservation can be enhanced far beyond this timescale owing to strong spin separation in quasi-momentum. This spin fidelity in long molecules is consistent with chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS), suggesting spin-dependent transport in long, chiral molecules with amplified spin-splitting mechanisms. We provide analytical derivation of the enhanced spin preservation and test it on tight-binding models, which confirm that the effect gradually weakens when the screw symmetry is broken or changes from discrete to continuous. Furthermore, we perform transport simulations to show that a strong magnetoresistance trace of symmetry-protected spin fidelity emerges in a spin-valve setup with two magnetic leads, which we propose as an experimentally accessible signature.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses cross-sectional STM to examine the atomic arrangement in (Al,Ga)Sb semiconductors. By directly counting atoms and analyzing electronic contrast, the researchers determined that the cation mixing is statistically random. This finding suggests the material possesses remarkable atomic-level homogeneity, consistent with either ideal mixing or weak, non-anisotropic interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily materials science, the analysis of local atomic order and the comparison to theoretical models (random vs. correlated) touches upon concepts of quenched disorder and local energy minimization, which are relevant to understanding complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the atomic-scale composition and cation mixing behavior within the ternary III-V semiconductor (Al,Ga)Sb.

**Main result.** The cation distribution was found to be statistically random both along the growth direction and within the growth plane, indicating an exceptionally high degree of atomic-level homogeneity.

**Method.** Cross-sectional Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (XSTM) was employed at 5 K, utilizing electronic contrast to perform direct atom counting on cleaved surfaces.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the (Al,Ga)Sb alloy grown by Molecular Beam Epitaxy (MBE). Analysis involves comparing experimental cation statistics against theoretical models, such as random occupancy or Markov-chain models accounting for strain-mediated interactions.

**Key observables.** Cation distribution statistics (randomness vs. correlation), mean number of consecutive cations of the same type ($\langle n angle$), and electronic contrast between Al and Ga surface cations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (5 K), semiconductor composition (Al/Ga ratio), and crystallographic directions ($\langle 110 angle$, $[001]$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed contrast is attributed to electronic differences (dangling-bond states) between Al and Ga atoms, and that statistical averaging over subsurface cations restores isotropy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relative orientations of cleavage planes, show cross-sections revealing chemical contrast between Al and Ga, and compare measured mean cation lengths ($\langle n angle$) against theoretical predictions from random and correlated models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by detailing the experimental setup (XSTM on specific planes), presenting the direct atom counting results, developing theoretical models (random vs. correlated mixing), and concluding by comparing the measured statistics against these models to establish the degree of homogeneity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cross-sectional scanning tunneling microscopy at 5 K is used to investigate cation mixing in (Al,Ga)Sb layers grown by molecular beam epitaxy, via direct atom counting at the (1-10) and (110) cleavage planes. Electronic contrast between Al and Ga surface cations enables statistical analysis of the metal sublattice along the non-equivalent <110> directions within the zincblende (001) surface and along the [001] growth direction. The cation distribution is found to be random both along the growth direction and within the growth plane, with no evidence of long-range order or anisotropic growth kinetics; notably, the mean numbers of consecutive cations of the same type along the two in-plane directions are equal, possibly due to statistical averaging over randomly distributed subsurface cations. The results are compatible with either strain-mediated interactions during cation incorporation at the growth front or ideal, uncorrelated cation mixing. Overall, the examined (Al,Ga)Sb alloy shows an exceptionally high degree of atomic-level homogeneity.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental study investigates the superconducting properties of the $	ext{Fe-Se-H}$ compound using EPR spectroscopy. By measuring magnetic field dependence at room temperature (295 K), the authors provide evidence for superconductivity and extrapolate the critical temperature to significantly higher values. The findings are significant for materials science due to the potential for room-temperature superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses high-$T_c$ superconductivity, a major topic in condensed matter physics, using advanced spectroscopic techniques to probe superconducting vortex dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish evidence for high-temperature superconductivity in the Fe-Se-H compound, aiming to increase the critical temperature ($T_c$) towards room temperature at normal pressure.

**Main result.** The $	ext{Fe-Se-H}$ compound exhibits superconductivity at normal pressure, confirmed by characteristic U-shaped magnetic-field dependences and quasi-periodic EPR signals observed up to 295 K.

**Method.** The study utilizes Electron Paramagnetic Resonance (EPR) spectroscopy to measure the dependence of microwave power absorption on magnetic field and temperature.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{Fe-Se-H}$ compound, formed by hydrogen diffusion into an $	ext{FeSe}$ single crystal. Measurements are performed on both powdered and single-crystal samples.

**Key observables.** The derivative of microwave power absorption ($	ext{dP/dH}$), U-shaped magnetic-field dependences (MFDS), and quasi-periodic oscillations in the EPR signal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperatures ranging from 3.6 K to 295 K; magnetic fields up to 6000 Oe; the calculated critical temperature extrapolation suggests $T_c \approx 340 	ext{ K}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quasi-periodic variations in the EPR signal are attributed to the presence of Abrikosov vortices, and the vortex area is approximated by $S_0 \approx \pi [\lambda(T)]^2/4$.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the U-shaped MFDS for both the single crystal $	ext{FeSe}$ and the $	ext{Fe-Se-H}$ powder at various temperatures, and high-resolution EPR signals demonstrating quasi-periodicity.

**Paper structure.** The paper presents measurements of microwave power absorption using EPR spectroscopy on $	ext{Fe-Se-H}$ across a wide temperature range. Evidence for superconductivity is drawn from the U-shaped MFDS and the analysis of quasi-periodic signals, leading to an extrapolation of the critical temperature.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using an EPR spectrometer, the dependences of microwave power absorption on magnetic field up to 6000 Oe were measured at temperatures of 3.6-25 K and 295 K in a powdered compound Fe-Se-H, obtained by thermal diffusion of hydrogen into a FeSe single crystal with a critical temperature of 8 K. The shape of the dependences and the analysis of the measurement results confirm the superconductivity of the Fe-Se-H compound at normal pressure, both at temperatures of 3.6-25 K and at 295 K, i.e., at room temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates the controlled, site-selective growth of $	ext{GaAs}_{1-x}	ext{Bi}_x$ shells on $	ext{GaAs}$ nanowire heterostructures containing alternating zinc blende and wurtzite phases. Using advanced electron microscopy, the researchers found that bismuth incorporation strongly depends on the crystal phase, favoring the Zb segments through an anion-exchange mechanism. This provides a pathway for engineering quantum materials with tailored functionalities.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on solid-state materials science, the precise control over crystal phase and atomic-scale material deposition is crucial for realizing novel quantum devices, linking to concepts of engineered quantum interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to engineer quantum materials by achieving site-selective nanostructure growth and material incorporation at the atomic scale.

**Main result.** Bi incorporation into GaAs nanowires is found to be crystal-phase-dependent, favoring the zinc blende (Zb) segments via an anion-exchange process, while the wurtzite (Wz) segments show higher susceptibility to oxidation.

**Method.** The research utilizes X-ray photoemission electron microscopy (XPEEM) and X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (XPS) to map elemental distribution and local chemical bonding across the heterostructure.

**Model / system.** The physical system consists of GaAs nanowires (NWs) with an axial heterostructure alternating between zinc blende (Zb) and wurtzite (Wz) crystal phases, which are used as templates for overgrowth.

**Key observables.** Elemental distribution ($	ext{Bi } 5d$, $	ext{Ga } 3d$, $	ext{As } 3d$), local chemical bonding, and crystal-phase-dependent Bi incorporation ratios.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The study highlights the difference in incorporation behavior between the Zb and Wz facets, and the resulting difference in oxidation susceptibility.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the observed differences in elemental signals reflect distinct chemical incorporation mechanisms (anion exchange vs. direct growth).

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematic representations of the NW growth, SEM images of the grown structures, and PEEM maps illustrating the spatial distribution of Bi and Ga signals across the Zb/Wz segments.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of establishing the need for site-selective growth, detailing the experimental setup using NW templates, presenting XPEEM/XPS data comparing Zb and Wz segments, and concluding with the mechanism of Bi incorporation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Site-selective nanostructure growth and material incorporation at the atomic scale offer a promising pathway for engineering quantum materials and nanodevices. Here, GaAs nanowires (NWs) with an axial heterostructure of alternating zinc blende (Zb) and wurtzite (Wz) crystal phases are employed as templates for site-selective Ga and Bi overgrowth. Using X-ray photoemission electron microscopy (XPEEM) with nanoscale spatial resolution, we map elemental distribution and local chemical bonding to reveal the incorporation behavior of Bi atoms in {110} Zb and {11-20} Wz facets. Bi incorporation proceeds through an anion-exchange process, where Bi atoms replace As, forming local Ga-Bi bonds and producing a thin GaAs1-xBix shell. We observe crystal-phase-dependent Bi incorporation, with higher Bi concentration in the Zb segments than in the neighboring Wz segments within the same NW. Furthermore, the Zb segment with higher Bi content exhibits reduced susceptibility to oxidation compared with the Wz segment, resulting in increased Ga-oxide in the Wz surfaces. This study highlights GaAs NW Zb/Wz heterostructures as a template for controlled growth of GaBi and GaAs1-xBix nanostructures with tailored functionalities for quantum applications

</details>

### [Beyond geometric symmetry: Broadband linear relations in wave scattering](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26562v1)

**Authors:** Malte Röntgen, Sucui Luo, Xuelong Chen, Chenyu Zhang, Ruotao Ye, Tianshu Jiang, Wenlong Gao  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.26562v1>
