**Summary.** This work introduces a method to find novel, broadband linear relations in wave scattering parameters that go beyond standard symmetries like reciprocity. By analyzing the effective Hamiltonian of waveguide networks, the authors show that the existence of a non-geometric symmetry guarantees such a linear relationship. This principle is demonstrated both theoretically and experimentally using coaxial cables.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of finding hidden, broadband symmetries in the effective Hamiltonian to predict linear relations in the scattering matrix is a powerful tool for designing novel wave devices, bridging concepts from symmetry theory to applied electromagnetics and photonics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To identify and utilize novel, broadband, linear relations between wave scattering parameters that cannot be derived from conventional physical symmetries like reciprocity or geometric symmetry.

**Main result.** The existence of a non-geometric symmetry, characterized by a commuting operator Q, guarantees the existence of a broadband linear relation between scattering parameters, which was experimentally verified.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving an effective Hamiltonian for wave scattering in waveguide networks and imposing the condition that this Hamiltonian commutes with a symmetry operator Q across a broad frequency range.

**Model / system.** The primary model is wave scattering in waveguide networks, implemented using coaxial cables, which are mathematically analogous to quantum graphs or RLC circuits. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian H_s(k) derived via techniques like the Schur complement.

**Key observables.** Scattering parameters (reflection $r_i$ and transmission $t_{ij}$), which are measured over a broad frequency range (0 to 1 GHz).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frequency (k), impedance mismatch ($\Gamma_L$), and the symmetry operator Q.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a broadband symmetry Q that commutes with the effective Hamiltonian H_s(k) for all relevant frequencies k.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate physical setups (coaxial cables, 3-port networks) and show the resulting scattering matrix relations for both symmetric and novel non-geometric cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by deriving the governing equations for wave propagation in complex networks, deriving the effective Hamiltonian H_s, establishing the link between symmetry in H_s and symmetry in the scattering matrix S_s, and finally presenting both theoretical derivations and experimental verification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The design and control of wave scattering, that is, of the reflection and transmission parameters of a device, is of ubiquitous importance. These parameters generally change with varying frequency, though certain \emph{frequency-independent} linear relations may exist between them. Reciprocity and geometric symmetry (reflections, rotations, etc.) are classic and well-known examples that are present in many devices and significantly ease their design. In this work, we go beyond these and introduce a new class of relations that cannot be induced by reciprocity or geometric symmetry. Choosing networks of waveguides as our workhorse, we discuss the conditions and consequences of such novel behaviour and showcase suitable example setups. We further experimentally test our predictions using coaxial cables and find excellent agreement in the broad frequency range between 0 and 1 GHz. Our work not only deepens the theoretical understanding of waveguide network dynamics, but also opens new avenues for applications in broadband signal processing, quantum information, and integrated photonics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses Terahertz Time-Domain Spectroscopy on $	ext{NiPS}_3$ to overcome limitations in separating magnetic and dielectric responses. By assuming the dielectric constant is stable upon cooling, the authors successfully isolate the magnetic permeability. This allowed them to distinguish between a temperature-dependent magnon mode and a symmetry-breaking zone-folded phonon, providing fundamental spin dynamics parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated experimental technique to probe coupled magnetic and lattice excitations in quantum materials, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phenomena in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to investigate the electrodynamic response of $	ext{NiPS}_3$ to decouple the magnetic permeability ($\mu$) from the electric permittivity ($\epsilon$) in the Terahertz (THz) range.

**Main result.** The research successfully identified a magnon mode near $1 	ext{ THz}$ (temperature-dependent) and a distinct zone-folded phonon activated by symmetry breaking (temperature-independent) using THz-TDS.

**Method.** The authors developed an extraction methodology using THz-TDS data, assuming the dielectric contribution ($\epsilon$) is invariant below the Néel temperature ($T_N$), allowing $\mu(\omega)$ to be isolated.

**Model / system.** The physical system is single-crystalline $	ext{NiPS}_3$, a layered Mott insulator exhibiting XY-type antiferromagnetic order. Measurements are performed using THz-TDS across different crystal orientations ($0^\circ$ and $90^\circ$) and temperatures.

**Key observables.** Complex permittivity ($\epsilon(\omega)$), complex permeability ($\mu(\omega)$), magnon mode resonance, and zone-folded phonon resonance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($13 	ext{ K}$ to $300 	ext{ K}$), THz frequency ($\sim 1 	ext{ THz}$), and crystallographic orientation ($0^\circ$ vs $90^\circ$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dielectric contribution ($\epsilon$) is assumed to remain invariant when cooling below the Néel temperature ($T_N$), and the magnetic permeability can be modeled using Drude-Lorentz oscillators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural characterization, anisotropic dielectric measurements, and dispersion plots showing temperature-dependent magnon shifts ($0^\circ$) versus constant phonon frequencies ($90^\circ$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the limitation of conventional THz analysis, introduces the decoupling methodology, presents THz-TDS measurements at varying temperatures and orientations, and finally models the isolated magnetic permeability using Drude-Lorentz oscillators to extract spin dynamics parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nickel phosphorus trisulfide (NiPS$_3$) stands out as a Mott insulator exhibiting XY-type antiferromagnetic order. In this work, we investigate the electrodynamic response of single-crystalline NiPS$_3$ using Terahertz Time-Domain Spectroscopy (THz-TDS) as a function of temperature. In cases where the magnetic permeability cannot be approximated to unity, conventional THz transmission analysis is often restricted to the complex refractive index, hindering the distinction between magnetic and dielectric contributions. To overcome this limitation, we demonstrate an extraction methodology capable of decoupling the magnetic permeability from the electric permittivity. Assuming that the dielectric contribution remains invariant below the N'eel temperature (TN), we used data from the paramagnetic phase to isolate the intrinsic magnetic component at 13 K. The angular and thermal dependence of the spectra revealed a magnon mode near 1 THz (for 0$^\circ$ orientation) and, distinctively, a zone-folded phonon activated by symmetry breaking (at 90$^\circ$). The isolated magnetic permeability was successfully modeled using Drude-Lorentz oscillators, enabling the extraction of fundamental spin dynamics parameters in this material.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses high-energy synchrotron X-ray diffraction combined with simulated annealing to precisely map the layer-by-layer structure of an AlGaAs/GaAs superlattice QBM. The analysis successfully quantified systematic growth errors, such as consistent thinning of barrier layers, providing critical, non-destructive feedback to improve the Molecular Beam Epitaxy growth process for high-performance quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is solid-state characterization, the rigorous application of advanced numerical optimization (SA) to extract precise, actionable structural parameters from complex scattering data is valuable for understanding material limitations in quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify nanoscale structural disorder in AlGaAs/GaAs superlattices used in Quantum Bragg Mirror (QBM) infrared detectors.

**Main result.** The analysis revealed systematic, material-specific thinning of AlGaAs barrier layers (0.3-0.6 nm) and significant deficits in the top and bottom contact layers, providing actionable feedback for MBE flux calibration.

**Method.** High-resolution structural characterization was performed using synchrotron X-ray diffraction, coupled with a recursive dynamical diffraction formalism and ensemble simulated annealing refinement.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a 203-layer AlGaAs/GaAs superlattice designed as a QBM. The analysis models the structural periodicity and composition to match experimental X-ray reflectivity data.

**Key observables.** Layer-by-layer thickness profiles, structural parameters ($ar{X}$), and the $\chi^2$ cost function value comparing simulated vs. experimental reflectivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Synchrotron X-ray energy (25 keV); diagnostic precision of 0.2 to 0.6 nm (1-2 atomic monolayers); systematic thickness deficits (e.g., 35 nm, 40 nm).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that structural disorder can be quantified by fitting the X-ray reflectivity curve, and the optimization is heavily weighted towards reproducing peak positions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the schematic of the nominal heterostructure. Figure 2 compares experimental and simulated X-ray reflectivity curves (Refined vs. Nominal). Figure 3 details the thickness profile within the active region.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need to quantify disorder, details the methodology combining dynamical diffraction theory with simulated annealing optimization, presents the systematic analysis of the 203-layer structure, and concludes with actionable feedback for improving MBE growth protocols.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

AlGaAs/GaAs superlattices grown by Molecular Beam Epitaxy (MBE) are foundational for advanced optoelectronic devices, including Quantum Bragg Mirror (QBM) infrared detectors. The performance of these devices critically depends on achieving near-perfect periodicity and abrupt interfaces, however, intrinsic statistical fluctuations during MBE growth introduce nanoscale structural disorder that can degrade device efficiency. In this study, we present a comprehensive methodology for quantifying this disorder in a 203-layer QBM device. High-resolution structural characterization was performed using high-energy (25 keV) synchrotron X-ray diffraction. By coupling a recursive dynamical diffraction formalism with an ensemble simulated annealing refinement, we extracted statistically robust, layer-by-layer thickness profiles. Our analysis reveals highly systematic, material-specific deviations from the nominal design: all AlGaAs barrier layers were consistently thinner than nominal by 0.3-0.6 nm. Furthermore, the sequential thickness profile successfully identified a significant 35 nm deficit in the final macroscopic top contact layer and a 40 nm deficit in the first GaAs layer. Achieving a statistical precision of about 0.2 to 0.6 nm (approximately 1-2 atomic monolayers), this non-destructive diagnostic approach provides directly actionable feedback for MBE flux calibration protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports observing a giant and switchable nonlinear Hall effect in double-ionic gated bilayer graphene at room temperature. By utilizing the ionic gating to break inversion symmetry, the authors achieved nonlinear susceptibilities significantly larger than previously reported room-temperature values. This positions bilayer graphene as a promising platform for next-generation, junction-free nonlinear electronic technology.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer large, switchable nonlinear responses at room temperature in a 2D material platform like graphene is highly relevant for developing novel quantum electronic components that operate outside cryogenic conditions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing materials with large and tunable nonlinearities at room temperature for practical, junction-free electronic devices.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a switchable, giant nonlinear Hall effect in double-ionic gated bilayer graphene, achieving susceptibilities comparable to low-temperature records.

**Method.** Measurements involved injecting alternating current at a fixed frequency and measuring both the second harmonic ($2\omega$) and fundamental ($\omega$) transverse and longitudinal voltages while sweeping gate voltages.

**Model / system.** The study uses double-ionic gated Bernal stacked bilayer graphene (BLG) devices. The ionic gating allows for precise control over the perpendicular electric field ($E_{BLG}$), which is crucial for breaking inversion symmetry.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Hall voltage ($V_{2\omega}^{xy}$), second-order nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi^{(2)}_{yxx}$), and THz voltage responsivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, perpendicular electric field strength ($E_{BLG}$), and the magnitude of $\chi^{(2)}_{yxx}$ ($\sim 3 	imes 10^{-3} \mu	ext{m S/V}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed nonlinear response is dominated by the second-order susceptibility and that the ionic gating mechanism provides the necessary symmetry breaking.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, the dependence of longitudinal resistance on gate voltages, and the sign change of the nonlinear transverse voltage ($V_{2\omega}^{xy}$) upon reversing the perpendicular electric field.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the measurement setup, confirms the nonlinear Hall effect's dependence on the perpendicular electric field, rules out trivial contributions (like the Berry curvature dipole), and quantifies the resulting large nonlinear coefficients.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Utilizing quantum second-order nonlinear transport for practical junction-free devices require materials with large and tunable nonlinearites at room temperature -- a current materials platform challenge. Here, we report the nonlinear Hall effect (NLHE) in double-ionic gated bilayer graphene devices that enable unusually strong inversion breaking. We observe NLHE that are readily switchable (on, off, and sign reversed) with second order nonlinear susceptibilities $χ^{(2)}_{yxx}$ that reaches giant room-temperature values of $3\,10^{-3}\,μ\mathrm{m}\,\mathrm{S/V}$, comparable to values commonly observed at low temperature in WTe$_2$ or in graphene-based moiré superlattices, and three-to-four orders of magnitude larger than values reported in material systems recently employed in search of a room-temperature NLHE. Our devices produce corresponding THz voltage responsivities $\simeq 4\,10^{4}\,\mathrm{V/W}$, comparable to commercially available Schottky diodes. These are orders of magnitude better than for previously reported room-temperature NLHE devices rendering double-ionic gated bilayer graphene a choice platform for junction-free nonlinear technology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the intricate geometry of nanostructures, exemplified by insect elytra, profoundly dictates their thermal behavior. By analyzing heat capacity measurements and thermal imaging, the authors show that geometric features can induce anomalous thermodynamic responses, such as negative apparent heat capacity, through structural relaxation pathways. This highlights geometry as a primary control knob for thermal energy management at the nanoscale.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit mathematical linkage between geometric curvature (a classical structural parameter) and the thermodynamic response (heat capacity anomaly) provides a powerful, non-chemical mechanism for controlling energy storage and dissipation, relevant to understanding complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how complex nanoscale geometries influence and shape the apparent thermodynamic response, particularly the heat-capacity, in materials like insect elytra.

**Main result.** Complex geometries can induce unusual, non-monotonic thermal responses, such as a decrease in apparent heat capacity, by modifying the energy barriers for internal relaxation mechanisms.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (DSC, thermal imaging) with theoretical modeling based on relaxation kinetics and geometric constraints.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the nanostructure of insect elytra, modeled using thermodynamic principles that link chemical potential changes to surface curvature ($\Delta\mu \propto 1/R_1 + 1/R_2$).

**Key observables.** Apparent heat capacity ($C_{app}$), temperature profiles under laser irradiation, and the rate-dependent shift of the heat-capacity anomaly.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Curvature ($R_1, R_2$), temperature range (20°C to 140°C), and the activation energy barriers ($E_i$) governing relaxation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The unusual thermal response is attributed to geometry-driven internal relaxation mechanisms rather than bulk phase transitions, and the model uses a coarse-grained description of structural rearrangements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the relationship between curvature and chemical potential, the experimental setup for thermal imaging, and the measured heat capacity curves showing non-monotonic behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the importance of nanoscale geometry in thermal management, presenting experimental evidence from insect elytra using DSC and laser thermography, and concluding with a theoretical model linking geometry to the apparent heat capacity anomaly via relaxation kinetics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

At the nano- and microscale, various patterns influence and shape thermal and optical response, making them essential for the survival of various biological species. In addition, controlling thermal radiation is vital for a broad range of applications, such as thermal management, spectroscopy, optoelectronics, and energy conversion technologies. For this reason, there is strong pressure to elucidate the physics of thermal radiation at the nanoscale. In this article, we provide evidence that complex nanoscale geometries affect thermal management, leading to an unusual thermal response in heat-capacity measurements as a function of temperature. Beyond identifying the structural constraints associated with this unusual thermodynamic response, the current study introduces the possibility of shaping the apparent heat-capacity response through geometry without necessarily altering the system's chemistry.

</details>
